- **Out-of-band rest-timer effects ride the singleton via two hooks, mounted once, by [RestTimerHost.jsx](../src/components/student/RestTimerHost.jsx), which [AppShell.jsx](../src/components/layout/AppShell.jsx) renders for a student (`role === 'student'`).** A change of tab does not end them — they used to be mounted by `SessionView`, and a look at Stats in the middle of a rest cancelled the push, cleared the beep and released the wake lock while the countdown ran on (B34). What ends a rest's effects is the timer (a new set, Undo, Skip, "+30 s", the end) or the shell unmounting at sign-out. Do not call the two hooks from a page. [hooks/useRestTimerEffects.js](../src/hooks/useRestTimerEffects.js) acquires `navigator.wakeLock('screen')` while a timer is active (the browser releases the lock by itself when the page is hidden, and a released `WakeLockSentinel` is a dead object: the sentinel's `release` event clears the handle, a sentinel whose `released` is `true` counts as no lock, and `visibilitychange → visible` requests a new one for as long as `Date.now() < endsAt`. A page hidden and shown while a request is in flight sends a second request; the one that resolves second is released at once, never stored over the first, because a sentinel that is not stored is never released and keeps the screen on after the rest), schedules a synthesized WebAudio beep + `navigator.vibrate` at `endsAt`, and mirrors `Rest m:ss · <title>` into `document.title` while the page is hidden so app-switcher glances show the countdown. The AudioContext is created/resumed on the first `pointerdown` (or `touchstart`) after the hook's first mount, so iOS lets it play unattended at expiry. The listener is armed by the hook (`armAudioUnlock`, once), never when the module loads: through `AppShell` the module is in the bundle a coach loads too, and a coach's first tap would build an AudioContext nobody plays. On iOS, Web Audio plays in the "ambient" audio session, which the ring/silent switch mutes; where `navigator.audioSession` exists, `playBeep` calls `takePlaybackSession`, which sets `navigator.audioSession.type` to `'playback'` for the beep, starts the beep 250 ms later (the type is applied in a task that runs after the assignment), and puts back the type it found 250 ms after the beep's last sample. **`'playback'` is taken for the beep only — never on the first `pointerdown`, never for the length of the session**: it is an exclusive type, and a page that holds it takes the phone's audio from the athlete's music instead of mixing with it. A type that is already `'playback'` is left to whoever set it, and nothing is written where the property does not exist. What the phone does with this is visible on an iPhone only; the tests in `useRestTimerEffects.test.js` hold when the property is written and that it is put back. [hooks/useRestTimerPush.js](../src/hooks/useRestTimerPush.js) is the Web Push bridge — see the next section. Neither hook reads or renders remaining time; they only react to `endsAt`/`logId` changes.

## Web Push (rest-timer "Rest done" lock-screen notification)

- **Live Activities / Dynamic Island are native-iOS-only.** PWA fallback is a Web Push notification fired at `endsAt`. Two tables back it: `push_subscriptions` (per-device subscription owned by the user; full CRUD via RLS keyed on `user_id = auth.uid()`, and `claim_push_subscription` to take a device over from the account that used it before — only with the keys stored with it) and `scheduled_pushes` (one row per rest whose end is to ring: `fire_at`, `payload`, `endpoint`, `status`, `canceled_at`, `sent_at`, `dispatched_at`). Both were added in [2026_05_11_push_notifications.sql](../supabase/migrations/2026_05_11_push_notifications.sql); there are no triggers on either. An account writes its own rows through RLS — the app from before wave 3 does — and since migration `2026_10_13_16_rest_push_scheduling.sql` four functions hold the rest push: the next bullets.
- **An endpoint is an address at a known push service, and the list is one list in four places.** `push_subscriptions.endpoint` carries the check `push_subscriptions_endpoint_known_push_service`: `https://`, then `fcm.googleapis.com`, `updates.push.services.mozilla.com`, a host under `push.apple.com` or one under `notify.windows.com`, then a path — lower case, no port, no user name. Without it a row is a way to make the Edge Functions POST to any URL (review B3). A refused write is `23514` and names the constraint, for every writer — the app's own upsert, `claim_push_subscription`, the service role. The same hosts are in `supabase/functions/_shared/pushHosts.js`, which the functions ask before they send, in the migration that last wrote the constraint, and in `supabase/tests/fixtures/push-endpoints.json`, which holds both sides to the same examples: a push service is added in all four, in one commit. A browser whose push service is not listed cannot switch notifications on.
- **A rest push is sent by whoever `claim_rest_push(id)` answers the row to, and by nobody else.** The function (migration `2026_10_13_16_rest_push_scheduling.sql`, service role only) moves the row from `pending` to `sending` and answers `{ id, user_id, payload, endpoint, fire_at }` — once. A second caller gets NULL, and so does a caller that asks for a row that was canceled or whose time is more than a minute past. There are two ways to send on purpose — the `dispatch-rest-push` Edge Function, and a pg_cron job through `send-push` — and an app from before wave 3 calls the Edge Function whatever the mode: the claim is what makes one notification of them. A sender asks at the time of the push, sends only what it was answered, then writes `sent` (and `sent_at`) or `failed` (and `error`) on that row. It never sends a row it merely read and never writes `sending` itself. `supabase/tests/rest-push.test.js` has the test on two connections.
- **A row is canceled when its status says so OR `canceled_at` is set.** The app from before wave 3 cancels with `UPDATE … SET canceled_at` and leaves the row `pending`; `cancel_rest_push` writes both. Whatever asks "is this row still to be sent" asks `status = 'pending' AND canceled_at IS NULL`.
- **The time of a rest push is the server's, and one account has one rest waiting.** `schedule_rest_push(p_seconds, p_payload, p_endpoint)` writes `fire_at = now() + p_seconds` — 1 to 900 seconds, in both modes — closes every row the caller still had `pending` (`canceled`; `expired` for one nobody had sent a minute after its time), deletes the caller's rows older than 7 days, and answers `{ id, fire_at, mode }`. Two calls of one account at the same instant are served one after the other. `mode` is `'cron'` only while `cron.job` holds an active job named `sl-rest-push`; otherwise `'edge'`, and the app must then call `dispatch-rest-push` with `{ scheduleId: id }` or nobody sends. Answering `'edge'` when the job does run costs one function call and never a second push.
- **The job hands a row over once, and leaves it `pending`.** `dispatch_due_rest_pushes()` — what the pg_cron job runs every ten seconds, and nothing else calls — passes each row that is waiting, not canceled, not handed over yet and due within ten seconds to `send_push_event` with `{ schedule_id, not_before, endpoint, ttl: 60, urgency: 'high' }` (`endpoint` left out when the row names none), and stamps `dispatched_at` in the same step. `send-push` waits until `not_before` and claims then, so a rest that ends early in those ten seconds is still taken back. Do not make the job claim, and do not make it hand a row over twice: a `send-push` that does not know `schedule_id` — the one from before wave 2 — sends at once and marks nothing, and would ring every ten seconds. **So the order of switching on is fixed: migration 16, then the two Edge Functions of wave 2, then pg_cron and migration 19.**
- **pg_cron is a choice of the owner, and everything works without it.** Production does not have the extension switched on. The optional migration `2026_10_13_19_pg_cron_jobs.sql` refuses to run without it (`SLM06`) and then makes four jobs, each of `postgres`, in this database: `sl-rest-push` (every 10 seconds, `dispatch_due_rest_pushes()`), `sl-retention` (every night at 03:17 UTC, `run_retention()`), `sl-push-failures` (every hour at :07, `record_push_failures()`) and `sl-cron-history` (every night, three days of pg_cron's own runs). Nothing in `src/` and no Edge Function may count on a job being there: the only thing that asks is `schedule_rest_push`, whose answer `mode` tells the app whether to call `dispatch-rest-push` itself. `supabase/tests/cron-jobs.test.js` holds the jobs; `docs/runbook/50-pg-cron.md` is the owner's page.
- **The name `sl-rest-push` is the contract.** `schedule_rest_push` answers `mode: 'cron'` while `cron.job` holds a job of that name that is switched on — whoever made it, whatever it runs — and `'edge'` otherwise: no extension, no job, a job switched off with `cron.alter_job(jobid, active := false)`. Never give that name to another job, and never make a job of that name by hand: the app of wave 3 stops calling the Edge Function for as long as it is there. A rest scheduled while the answer was `'cron'` is sent by the job and by nobody else, so switching the job off — a pause, the rollback of migration 19, the extension switched off in the dashboard — leaves the rests that are running at that instant without a sender (15 minutes of them at most); the app from before wave 3 calls the Edge Function for every rest and is not concerned.
- **The job needs the `send-push` of wave 2, and nothing in the database can check that.** It hands a row over ten seconds before its time with `schedule_id` and `not_before`; a `send-push` that does not know them sends at once, to every device, and the function the app called sends again at the time. The order of switching on is migration 16, the two Edge Functions of wave 2, the extension, migration 19 — written on both runbook pages, and held by nothing but them.
- **More than a minute late is never sent.** A row still `pending` 60 seconds after `fire_at` is closed as `expired` (`canceled` when `canceled_at` is set) — by the job, by `claim_rest_push` when it is asked for that row, by the next rest of its account. `interval '60 seconds'` is written in those three functions; a change is a change of all three. `expired` rows are the measure of rest pushes nobody sent; a row left `sending` is a sender that claimed and never reported.
- **A rest push is gone a week after it was written.** `scheduled_pushes` is the queue of the rests that are to ring, not a record of the rests an athlete took: `schedule_rest_push` deletes the caller's own rows older than 7 days when they next rest, and `run_retention()` (migration `2026_10_13_18_retention.sql`) deletes everybody's — also the rows the app from before wave 3 inserts itself, which nothing else deletes. Whatever became of the row: `sent`, `failed`, `canceled`, `expired`, left `sending`. The one exception is a row that is still to be sent, which only a phone whose clock is days ahead can leave behind. Nothing may count on a row of that table older than a week; a statistic about rests reads `set_logs`.
- **An account names a device of its own.** `scheduled_pushes.endpoint` is where the push goes: one of the caller's `push_subscriptions`, or NULL for every device of the account. `schedule_rest_push` refuses anything else (`device_not_registered`), and the `INSERT` and `UPDATE` policies of the table ask the same of a row written directly. A sender reads the devices of the row's `user_id`, then filters by the endpoint — never by the endpoint alone: a device that changed hands since is no longer that account's.
- **The app schedules a rest push in one request, for this device, and falls back only when the functions are missing.** [hooks/useRestTimerPush.js](../src/hooks/useRestTimerPush.js) calls `schedule_rest_push(p_seconds, p_payload, p_endpoint)`: `p_seconds` the whole seconds left, an integer (`Math.ceil`, counted right before the request leaves — a fraction is a `22P02`), `p_endpoint` the endpoint of this device's subscription (`getActivePushSubscription`), never NULL — without it every device of the account rings for a rest taken on one of them (B38). Nothing is asked without a signed-in user, without push support, without the permission `granted`, without a subscription, offline (`navigator.onLine === false`: the server would count the seconds from whenever the request arrived) or for a rest outside 1–900 s. With `mode: 'cron'` the app does nothing more; with any other answer — `'edge'`, or no `mode` at all — it invokes `dispatch-rest-push` with `{ scheduleId: id }`, fire and forget: a call too many costs nothing, since every sender claims. A rest that changes or ends — Undo, Skip, the next set, "+30 s", the shell unmounting — takes its push back with `cancel_rest_push(id)`, and a rest that ends while its push is being scheduled takes it back as soon as the id is known and never dispatches it. The schedule requests leave one after the other, whichever mount of the hook sends them: each waits for the one before to be answered, `PREVIOUS_REQUEST_WAIT_MS` (5 s) at most, and a rest that changed during the wait asks for nothing — `schedule_rest_push` closes every row the account has waiting, so an old rest's request overtaken by the next one's would close the new row and then be taken back itself. **Only `PGRST202` falls back** to the insert of the app from before wave 3 (`user_id`, `fire_at` from the phone's clock, `payload`, no `endpoint` key) and its `UPDATE … SET canceled_at … WHERE status = 'pending'`; a refusal (`device_not_registered`, `rest_seconds_out_of_range`, `payload_invalid`, `42501`) or no answer schedules nothing, because the old insert names no device. The payload is `{ title, body, tag: REST_TIMER_TAG, data: { url } }`, written with `getMessage` in the language of the app at the moment the push is scheduled (`student.session.restDone`, `restPush.body`) — the language is followed through a ref, so changing it during a rest neither cancels nor reschedules the push. `data.url` is `${BASE_URL}#/student/session/<id>`, the session of the set (`logId`), found in the lists of sets the device holds (`['set-logs', sessionId, slotIds]`) without a request; when none holds the set the payload has no `data` and a tap moves no window. **Never `#/student`**: a tap on it took the athlete out of the session they were resting in (P50). The hook does not read the route: `student/RestTimerHost` mounts it with the student shell, on every page. Tests: `hooks/useRestTimerPush.test.jsx`.
- **Custom service worker, not generated.** `vite-plugin-pwa` runs in `injectManifest` mode against [src/sw.js](../src/sw.js); the generated SW is gone. The custom SW still precaches the app shell + Google Fonts via workbox (so offline behaviour is unchanged) and listens to six events. Five of them are one line each, which calls a function of [src/lib/swHandlers.js](../src/lib/swHandlers.js) with the worker's scope: `push` → `handlePush`, `notificationclick` → `handleNotificationClick`, and `install`, `activate`, `message` → the update flow's three (see "App updates"). What the worker does is in that file, where vitest runs it (`src/lib/swHandlers.test.js`); `src/sw.test.js` loads the real `sw.js` and holds the wiring. `pushsubscriptionchange` is still written in `sw.js` itself. Workbox is pulled in transitively by `vite-plugin-pwa`; don't add it to `package.json` explicitly.
- **Every push shows a notification. Never skip `showNotification`, for any reason — an open app included.** A push whose handler shows nothing is a silent push, which a subscription made with `userVisibleOnly` does not allow: WebKit counts them and takes the subscription away, and the device gets no notification at all until its owner turns them on again. So `handlePush` has no early return, and three things in it exist only for this rule: `readPushPayload` never throws (text that is not JSON becomes the body, JSON that is not an object and a payload that cannot be read are still shown, under the name of the app); the tag is never empty (`renotify` without a tag is a `TypeError`, and a `showNotification` that throws has shown nothing); and the one question asked before the notification is shown — is the app in front? — is asked for the tag `REST_TIMER_TAG` only and has `IN_FRONT_ANSWER_MS` to be answered, after which the push is shown the usual way. A condition in front of `handlePush` in `sw.js` breaks the rule just as well; `src/sw.test.js` shows a "Rest done" to a focused window for that reason.
- **"Rest done" in front of the athlete is shown quietly and taken away — the tag `rest-timer` is what says which push that is.** The page that runs a rest beeps and vibrates at its end by itself ([hooks/useRestTimerEffects.js](../src/hooks/useRestTimerEffects.js)), so the system banner of the push is a second cue for the same thing. When a push tagged `REST_TIMER_TAG` arrives while a window of the app is focused and not hidden (`appIsInFront`), `handlePush` shows it with `notificationFor(payload, { quiet: true })` — `silent: true`, `renotify: false` and **no `vibrate` key at all**: `silent` together with `vibrate` is a `TypeError` — and closes it `FOCUSED_REST_CLOSE_MS` later (`closeNotifications`, by tag) — unless another "Rest done" has been shown since. The tag is also the one of a "Rest done" that replaced the quiet one in those seconds, and that one may be loud (the phone went into a pocket, the next rest ended): the worker counts the "Rest done" it asks `showNotification` for (`restShows`), a quiet one keeps the number it was shown under, and it is closed only while that number is still the last. The one that replaced it is its own push's to close, if quiet, or to leave, if loud. The count is taken when a notification is asked for, not when a push arrives: a "Rest done" still waiting for its answer about the windows has replaced nothing, and the quiet one before it goes on time. `silent` is a request a browser may ignore; the close is the part that does not depend on it. A browser that refuses the quiet form gets the usual one, which stays. Every other tag is shown as usual whatever the app is doing, and so is a "Rest done" with the app in the background, with no window, or with a window that is visible but not focused. **Whoever writes the payload of the rest push keeps the tag `rest-timer`** ([hooks/useRestTimerPush.js](../src/hooks/useRestTimerPush.js) writes `REST_TIMER_TAG`): under another tag the push is an ordinary one — two cues in front of the athlete, and a notification nothing closes. The value cannot follow a rename either: the client deployed before wave 3 writes the literal, and `scheduled_pushes` holds payloads written by it (`lib/swHandlers.test.js` pins it). `hooks/useRestTimerPush.test.jsx` hands the payload the hook really writes to `handlePush` and `handleNotificationClick` and fails when the worker does not treat it as the rest push. "In front" relies on what makes the push arrive there at all: the app that schedules it is the app that beeps — `RestTimerHost` runs both, on every page of the student shell — and the push is taken back when the rest is cleared, replaced, skipped or given 30 s more (a new push is scheduled for the new end), or when the shell unmounts at sign-out. A change of tab keeps it.
- **A tap brings the app to the front and moves a window only when it is somewhere else.** `handleNotificationClick` reads the address of the payload against the scope of the registration (`targetOf` — absolute, and `null` for a payload without `data.url`, with one that cannot be read, or with an address outside the app) and compares it with what each window reports through `isSameRoute`: same origin, same path — where a path that ends in a whole `index.html` segment is its directory, so `/sl_app/index.html` and `/sl_app/` are one document and `/sl_app/myindex.html` is not — same route in the hash, where a trailing `/` does not count and `#/`, `#` and no hash are one route. The two are never compared as text — a window reports `https://host/sl_app/#/…` and a payload carries `/sl_app/#/…`, which never match, and every tap then loads the page again under its user. A window already on the page is focused and left as it is, and is preferred to any other; a window elsewhere is focused, then sent there with `navigate` (an error there is ignored: the focus is the part that is guaranteed); a window that cannot be focused is passed over for the next; with none, `openWindow` opens the address, or the app itself when the payload names none. A payload without an address focuses and moves nothing. **The payload decides where a tap leads — except that a "Rest done" never takes the athlete out of a session to a page that is not one.** `handleNotificationClick` passes the tag of the tapped notification to `bringAppToFront`: for a notification tagged `REST_TIMER_TAG` whose address is not a session — the training list, which the client deployed before wave 3 and every row it scheduled give it — a window on a session (`/student/session/<id>`, `isSessionRoute`) is focused and never navigated, and is preferred to a window that would have to move. A window on the payload's own page still comes first, a window on another page is still sent to the address, and with no window the address is opened. A "Rest done" that names its session (what [hooks/useRestTimerPush.js](../src/hooks/useRestTimerPush.js) writes since wave 3) is read as it is: a window on another session is sent to it. Every other tag follows its payload alone. The tag is read from the notification, synchronously: nothing more is awaited before `matchAll`. **Only windows of the app count** (`appWindows`: `client.url` starts with `registration.scope`): `matchAll({ includeUncontrolled: true })` lists every window of the origin, and `<account>.github.io` is shared with whatever else is published there — a tab of another site is neither "the app in front" nor a window to send somewhere. The window is asked for first and nothing is awaited before it: a worker may focus or open a window only for a short while after the tap.
- **A "Rest done" that is no longer news is closed when the app comes back — by the worker on a tap, by the page otherwise.** The tag makes the next rest replace the notification of the one before; nothing replaces the last one of a session. A tap on any notification closes the ones tagged `REST_TIMER_TAG` (`handleNotificationClick`). The worker hears nothing when the app is opened from its icon or from the app switcher, so the page does it: [src/lib/restNotifications.js](../src/lib/restNotifications.js) — `restNotifications.install()` in [src/main.jsx](../src/main.jsx), production builds only — calls `closeRestNotifications` once when it is installed on a visible page, at each `visibilitychange` to visible and at each `focus` of the window (a desktop window that stayed visible only gets the second). It asks nothing where `Notification.permission` is not `granted`, and uses `getRegistration()`, never `navigator.serviceWorker.ready`, which does not answer on a page no worker is registered for. A sweep waits `SWEEP_ANSWER_MS` (5 s) at most for the device (`within` of [src/lib/swHandlers.js](../src/lib/swHandlers.js), the bound the worker puts on its own question about the windows): the watcher's `running` flag, which makes the `visibilitychange` and the `focus` of one return a single sweep, is cleared then, so a `getRegistration()` or a `getNotifications()` that never answers costs one sweep and not every later one.
- **Two Edge Functions handle delivery, split by call shape.** [dispatch-rest-push](../supabase/functions/dispatch-rest-push/index.ts) is **called by the app, and by itself** — the client posts `{ scheduleId }` with its own JWT; the function checks the token with the auth server, loads the `scheduled_pushes` row with the service role and asks whose it is, answers `202`, and under `EdgeRuntime.waitUntil` waits a minute at most. A longer rest is handed to a new request to its own address, which shows the `INTERNAL_BEARER` secret and no user token; the last request asks `claim_rest_push` and sends what the claim answered. [send-push](../supabase/functions/send-push/index.ts) is **internal-only** — it requires `Authorization: Bearer <INTERNAL_BEARER>`, a secret of its own that is not the service-role key, and is called by the notification triggers via pg_net. It takes `{ user_id, payload }` — plus `ttl` and whatever options `send_push_event` was given, at the top level of the body — and fans out. Both share the web-push + VAPID stack; 404/410 from the push service drops the dead subscription in either function.
- **One function hands a push to `send-push`, and every notification asks it.** `public.send_push_event(p_user, p_payload, p_options)` (migration `2026_10_13_15_push_plumbing.sql`) is the only function of `public` that reads the vault or calls `net.http_post`: the address is `app_functions_url` followed by `/send-push`, the bearer is `app_internal_bearer` — and `app_service_role_key` while the vault has nothing under that name —, the time limit is 10 seconds, and the body is `{ user_id, payload, ttl: 86400 }` plus the keys of `p_options` at the top level. It answers the id pg_net gave the request, or NULL when the vault lacks the address or the bearer: nothing is queued then and nothing is raised, so the feature degrades cleanly where push is not set up. The six notification triggers and the five coach RPCs call it inside a block of their own, after their bell row: `BEGIN PERFORM public.send_push_event(…); EXCEPTION WHEN OTHERS THEN RAISE WARNING '… failed: %', SQLERRM; END;` — a push that cannot be queued never fails the write that caused it. **A new function that notifies does the same and joins `LATER_SENDERS` in `supabase/tests/push-plumbing.test.js`**, which reads the text of every function of `public`: a second one that reads the vault or calls pg_net, or a call outside such a block, fails it.
- **`send_push_event` is for the owner and the service role, and has no right of its own.** It is `SECURITY INVOKER` and not in the allow-list: whoever may call it sends any text and any link to any account. Its callers are `SECURITY DEFINER`, so it runs as their owner, who reads the vault. Called from a function that runs with a signed-in user's rights it is refused, and the caller's `EXCEPTION` block turns the refusal into a warning — no push, and no error on the screen. Do not grant it to `authenticated`, and do not make it `SECURITY DEFINER`.
- **Nobody reads what `send-push` answers.** pg_net sends the request after `COMMIT`, once, and the database never looks at the response: a warning in the log means the request could not be queued, not that a push failed to arrive. The bell row is the record. The 10 seconds are for the function's answer; pg_net's own default is 2 or 5 seconds depending on its version, which a cold start does not fit in.
- **The secret `send-push` checks is `INTERNAL_BEARER`, and the vault holds the same value.** It is a random string made for this purpose — never the service-role key, whatever the older vault name `app_service_role_key` suggests — because whoever has it can push any text and any link to any user. Supabase's auto-injected `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` is not reused for the check either: newer projects get an `sb_secret_*` key whose full value cannot be read back, so the two ends would drift. `ALTER DATABASE postgres SET app.*` is denied on Supabase's managed Postgres — don't reach for GUCs. The vault's copy is changed with `vault.update_secret(id, '<new>')`, never with `UPDATE vault.secrets`. Rotation is the owner's, by hand (`docs/runbook/30-edge-functions.md`, section 4, whose statements `supabase/tests/push-secrets.test.js` reads off the page and runs); renaming the secret to `app_internal_bearer` is optional and is on `docs/runbook/migrations/15-push-plumbing.md`. With both names in the vault, `app_internal_bearer` is the one that is sent.
- **`supabase/config.toml` says, for every Edge Function, whether the gateway asks for a user JWT.** `verify_jwt = false` for both: each is also called by the project itself, which has no user JWT to show — send-push by the database, dispatch-rest-push by its own hops. With `true` in front of dispatch-rest-push the app's request still gets through, and every request the function makes to itself is refused by the gateway: a rest of a minute or less rings and no longer one does, with nothing to show for it but a line in the function's log. The deploy is a plain `supabase functions deploy <name> --use-api`; `--no-verify-jwt` is never passed by hand, because the flag overrides the file. Without the file a deploy that forgets the flag puts the gateway's 401 in front of send-push, and every push of the app stops with no error anywhere: the triggers never read the answer. `supabase/functions/deploy.test.js` fails when a function directory has no `[functions.<name>]` table, when `verify_jwt` is not written out, or when the flags are not the ones in production. The file carries nothing else (`src/test/authConfig.test.js`), and `supabase config push` stays forbidden.
- **A function the gateway does not guard judges its caller before anything else, and nothing runs for a caller it refuses. One that only the project calls opens with `admitInternalRequest`.** `supabase/functions/_shared/auth.js` is the whole gate: `POST` only (a preflight gets a 405 and no `Access-Control-*` header, so a browser cannot call the function), the bearer compared with `timingSafeEqual` (never `===`, whose duration tells how long the matching prefix is), a body of `MAX_BODY_BYTES` at most, counted while it is read, and a JSON object. An unset secret refuses everyone with a 500 — `"Bearer undefined"` must never open anything. A refusal is plain text that starts with the function's name (`send-push: unauthorized`): the check of the runbook tells the function's refusal from the gateway's by that text, so do not reword it without the page. Do not add CORS headers to an internal function, and do not read the body before the bearer is judged.
- **`dispatch-rest-push` has two ways in, and the header decides which one is judged.** `authenticate` in `supabase/functions/_shared/restPush.js` runs before a byte of the body is read and before a client is made. A request that carries `x-internal-bearer` is a hop: the value is compared with `INTERNAL_BEARER` by `checkSecret` (constant time), a wrong or empty one is a 401 whatever else the request shows, and an unset secret is a 500 for a hop only — the app is still served. Any other request is the app's: `Authorization: Bearer <token>`, refused without a call when it is not shaped like a JWT, otherwise handed to the auth server through `auth.getUser(token)` with the anon key; a 4xx is a 401, no answer is a 503. The service-role client is made only for a caller that was let in, and the function then asks `row.user_id === caller.userId` itself — the client that reads the row holds every row. A hop has no user: its first request was asked. Never accept the secret in `Authorization` or a token in `x-internal-bearer`, and never fall back from one to the other.
- **`dispatch-rest-push` answers a browser, and its CORS headers do not allow `x-internal-bearer`.** The app calls it from its page, so `OPTIONS` is answered and every answer carries `CORS_HEADERS` — unlike send-push, which gives a browser nothing. `Access-Control-Allow-Headers` is `authorization, x-client-info, apikey, content-type`: adding the internal header would let any page try the secret from a visitor's browser.
- **`401 dispatch-rest-push: unauthorized` is read by the runbook's check.** A refusal is plain text that starts with the function's name. `docs/runbook/30-edge-functions.md` (section 3) sends one request that shows nothing and tells that line from the gateway's JSON, which means the function is deployed with JWT verification on; `supabase/functions/runbook.test.js` runs the command off the page against `dispatch-rest-push/index.ts`. Do not reword it without the page.
- **A push that was not taken is a line of the log, and a request nobody took is a 502.** `sendToDevices` in `supabase/functions/_shared/push.js` is the one place that calls `webpush.sendNotification`: for every device that did not take the message it writes `<function>: push failed status=<code|none> host=<host> subscription=<row id> body="…"` (`error="…"` when the push service did not answer), and `send-push` answers `502 {"delivered":0,"failed":N}` when the user had devices and none took it for another reason than being gone. `200 {"delivered":N}` otherwise — also `200 {"delivered":0}` for a user without a device and for one whose every device answered 404 or 410: those rows are deleted, the line is a warning, and no device is not an error. Nobody reads the answer at the time, so the log line is the trace: do not catch a send without writing it, and do not answer 200 for "nobody got it". **The line names the host and never the rest of the endpoint**, which is the device's own address; `pushHost()` gives the host, and an answer that echoes the address or a key has it removed. `docs/runbook/30-edge-functions.md` (section 5) quotes the lines, and `supabase/functions/runbook.test.js` reproduces each one through `send-push/index.ts`: reword a line on the page and in the code together.
- **Every send carries its options: how long the message is kept, how urgent it is, and a time limit.** `sendOptions(body, fallback)` reads `ttl` (seconds) and `urgency` (`very-low`, `low`, `normal`, `high`) from the request — `send_push_event` puts them at the top level of the body — and falls back to one day and `normal` for an event, one minute and `high` for a rest push. A value the push services would not take is replaced by the fallback with a warning: the web-push library refuses to send at all when handed one, and a notification is worth more than an option. Without options the library keeps a message four weeks. Each send has the library's 5 second limit (`SEND_TIMEOUT_MS`) and is over after 8 (`SEND_DEADLINE_MS`) whatever the library does: pg_net waits 10 seconds for the function.
- **The functions send to an address of the list and nowhere else.** `sendToDevices` asks `isPushEndpoint` (`supabase/functions/_shared/pushHosts.js`) before each send. An endpoint that is not at one of the four push services is not called: it is logged (`push not sent host=…`), counted as a failed device, and its row is kept — only a push service's 404 or 410 deletes a row. The function does not rely on the check constraint of `push_subscriptions`: rows that predate it are still read. `supabase/functions/_shared/pushHosts.test.js` holds `PUSH_ENDPOINT_PATTERN` to the constraint in `supabase/schema.sql`, character for character, and both to `supabase/tests/fixtures/push-endpoints.json`.
- **A request to `send-push` that names a row is a rest push: answered 202, sent after the claim, with what the claim answered.** `schedule_id` in the body — whatever its value — takes the request out of the fan-out: it is never sent at once to every device. The function refuses a `schedule_id` that is not a uuid and a `not_before` that is not a time or is more than 120 seconds ahead (`MAX_WAIT_MS`: a function may run for 150 seconds), answers `202 {"accepted":true}`, and under `EdgeRuntime.waitUntil` waits until `not_before` and calls `sendRestPush`: `claim_rest_push(schedule_id)`, then the devices of the CLAIM's `user_id` (filtered by the claim's `endpoint` when it names one), the claim's `payload`, and `sent` or `failed` written on the row — `no_subscriptions`, `all_subscriptions_failed`, or `sender_failed` when something broke after the claim, because a claimed row that nobody closes reads `sending` for ever. A claim that answers nothing, or that cannot be asked, sends nothing and writes nothing. The `user_id`, `payload` and `endpoint` of the request are not used for a rest push. `sendRestPush` is what any sender of a rest push calls at the time of the push; it never rejects.
- **A rest is waited for a minute at a time (`HOP_MS`), not for the 150 seconds a function may run.** The 150 seconds of the free plan are a worker's, not a request's: a worker of Supabase's runtime takes every request that arrives during the first half of its life and retires at 75 seconds, so a request can land on a worker that is 75 seconds old and is promised the other 75. A hop is a wait of 60 seconds at most, then the claim and the push (`SEND_DEADLINE_MS`, 8 s, plus `TOP_UP_MAX_MS`) or the hand-over (`HANDOVER_DEADLINE_MS`, 10 s). `planHop(remainingMs)` in `supabase/functions/_shared/restPush.js` is the whole plan: a rest takes the fewest requests that fit (`ceil(remaining / HOP_MS)`: three for 180 s, five for 300 s, fifteen for 900 s), no wait is longer than a minute, and a hop that hands over leaves the next one `LAST_HOP_MIN_MS` (30 s) so that a worker that has to start never takes its start from the time of the push. Calls from a function to a function of its project are counted by the platform — 30 in 60 seconds for one chain, then the call throws: a rest makes two in a minute at most. Raising `HOP_MS` above what half a worker's life leaves loses rests whenever two requests reach one worker — two athletes, or one athlete who re-ticks a set — and no test of the repository can see it.
- **A hop is a request the function makes to its own address, with the secret and nothing of the athlete.** `handOver` posts `{ scheduleId, hop }` to `functionUrl(SUPABASE_URL, name)` with `content-type` and `x-internal-bearer`, `redirect: 'manual'` and a time limit. It never forwards the user's token — which can run out in the middle of a rest, and which the next hop does not need. One attempt: there is no retry and no sweeper, a hand-over that fails is a rest that does not ring and one `error` line of the log (`hop N did not take over, the rest does not ring: <why>`). Each hop reads the row again when it starts and computes its wait from `fire_at`, so the seconds a hand-over takes are not added to the rest; before it hands over it reads the row once more and stops the chain when the rest is no longer waiting (`status` other than `pending`, or `canceled_at` set — the app from before wave 3 cancels by writing `canceled_at` alone). A stopped chain writes nothing on the row. `MAX_HOPS` ends a chain that would not end; the number of a hop is believed from a hop only.
- **`dispatch-rest-push` sends through `sendRestPush`, at the time of the push by its own clock.** Its last hop waits what is left, looks at the clock once more — a timer counts its own seconds; `claim_rest_push` does not refuse a row that is early — and then asks the claim. It never sends a row it has merely read and never writes `sending`, `canceled` or `expired` itself: those are the claim's. A row more than `MAX_AHEAD_MS` ahead (the 900 s of `schedule_rest_push` plus half a minute for two clocks that disagree) is refused with a 422 and left as it is.
- **`dispatch-rest-push` of wave 2 needs migration 16, and is deployed before the job `sl-rest-push` exists.** Without `claim_rest_push` it sends nothing (`claim_rest_push could not be asked, nothing is sent`). The version before it (v2) sends without asking: next to the job, both send. `docs/runbook/30-edge-functions.md` ("When", "Undo") has the order; the function reads only columns `scheduled_pushes` has had since it was made, so the read itself works on either side of the migration.
- **The lines of the log of `dispatch-rest-push` are quoted by the runbook.** `docs/runbook/30-edge-functions.md` (section 5) shows what a rest of three minutes writes, request by request, and what it writes where the gateway refuses a hop; `supabase/functions/runbook.test.js` runs that rest through `dispatch-rest-push/index.ts` and compares to the character. Reword a line on the page and in `restPush.js` together.
- **The adapter reads `EdgeRuntime` when a request comes in, from `globalThis`.** Deno does not know the name: a bare `EdgeRuntime` fails `deno check`, and one read at module load is `undefined` under vitest. Without a runtime to hand the work to, `answerPush` does the work before it answers — late for the caller, and sent.
- **`send-push` of wave 2 is deployed before the job `sl-rest-push` exists, and is not replaced by an older version while the job runs.** A `send-push` that does not know `schedule_id` sends what the job hands it at once, to every device, ten seconds early. The two versions have the same gate and cannot be told apart without the secret: the third line of the check in `docs/runbook/30-edge-functions.md` (section 3), sent only when the shell holds `INTERNAL_BEARER`, is a rest push whose id is not an id — this version answers `400 send-push: schedule_id is not a uuid`, the one before answers `200 {"delivered":0}`. Do not reword that refusal without the page.
- **What an Edge Function decides lives in `supabase/functions/_shared/*.js`; `index.ts` is an adapter.** A shared module uses web standards only (`Request`, `Response`, streams) — no `Deno.*`, no `jsr:` or `npm:` import — and takes its dependencies as arguments, so vitest runs it under Node (`// @vitest-environment node`). Deno is not installed where the tests run. The adapter reads the environment and wires things together, and is tested too: `supabase/functions/_test/harness.js` (`loadFunction`) imports an `index.ts` with a stand-in `Deno` global and its remote imports replaced, and returns the handler it passed to `Deno.serve`. A shared module is imported by a relative path with its extension (`'../_shared/auth.js'`): `supabase functions deploy --use-api` uploads the files it finds by matching the `import` lines with a regular expression, comments included, and `supabase/functions/deploy.test.js` runs that expression over every function to see that it finds each file and nothing else.
- **A shared module hands out its names in one `export { … };` list at its end, and never puts a quoted text after `import` or `export` on a line.** The expression with which `supabase functions deploy --use-api` finds the files to upload takes any quoted text that follows one of the two words on the same line for a file: `export const REASON = 'no_subscriptions'`, a default argument that is a string on an exported function, a comment that quotes an import. `supabase/functions/deploy.test.js` fails with "the CLI would take '…' for an import"; `supabase/functions/_shared/push.js` and `pushHosts.js` show the form that passes — constants on `const` lines, one list at the end.
- **Nothing in the repository deploys a function or sets a secret.** The owner does, following `docs/runbook/30-edge-functions.md`. The check that page gives for after a deploy is read off the page and run by `supabase/functions/runbook.test.js` against `send-push/index.ts` itself, behind a real port, and against what a wrong deploy answers; it sends the secret only to something that has first answered as send-push.
- **A rest is 1 to 900 seconds for the database, and the app asks for nothing outside that.** `schedule_rest_push` refuses a rest outside 1–900 seconds (`rest_seconds_out_of_range`) in both modes: 15 minutes is what the Edge Function can wait for, in hops, where no job runs. [hooks/useRestTimerPush.js](../src/hooks/useRestTimerPush.js) asks for a rest of 1 to `MAX_REST_PUSH_SECONDS` (900) seconds left, and for nothing otherwise — a longer rest keeps the in-app beep and the wake lock. The 350 s clamp is only in the client deployed before wave 3, which inserts its row itself; with the `dispatch-rest-push` deployed before wave 2, which waited inside one request the platform ends after 150 seconds, a rest of 150 seconds or more got no push.
- **VAPID keys are environment-scoped.** Public key ships to the client at build time via `VITE_VAPID_PUBLIC_KEY` (`.env`). Private key + a `mailto:` subject live in Supabase function secrets (`VAPID_PRIVATE_KEY`, `VAPID_SUBJECT`). Generate once with `npx web-push generate-vapid-keys --json` and set via `supabase secrets set ...`. If `VITE_VAPID_PUBLIC_KEY` is empty, `isPushSupported()` reports false and the Profile toggle renders the "not supported" copy instead of attempting to subscribe.
- **iOS support requires the PWA be installed.** Web Push on iOS Safari is gated on iOS 16.4+ AND the user adding the app to the Home Screen. We don't try to detect installation explicitly — `PushManager.subscribe` will throw and the toggle surfaces the error. Don't bypass `userVisibleOnly: true` (Apple rejects silent pushes).
- **The device's subscription is written to the database at most once an hour, except when it has to be.** [`usePushAutoHeal`](../src/hooks/usePushAutoHeal.js) looks on load and on every return to the tab, as before, but each look used to be a write of `push_subscriptions` — one per unlock of a workout. It now writes when the last write for this user is older than `HEAL_EVERY_MS`, and at once in three cases the hour must not delay: another account on this device (the stamp names the user), an account that is back on a device it signed out of with notifications on (`isPushResumable`), and an endpoint that is no longer the one last written — the worker re-subscribes on `pushsubscriptionchange` but only the page knows who is signed in, and until the row has the new address every push to this device fails. The stamp is `localStorage['sl_push_heal']` = `{ userId, endpoint, at, ok, code }` — the last look made from this device: for which account, about which address of the device, when, whether the database took it, and when it did not, the code of what failed, stored and not kept in memory because iOS closes an installed app behind the lock screen, which would make "once on load" once per unlock again. A look that failed is noted with `ok: false`, its `code` and the address it was about, and tried again after `HEAL_RETRY_MS` (5 minutes), not at every unlock — nothing is thrown and nobody is told at that moment, but the failure is no longer forgotten: `lastHeal(userId)` hands it to the notification toggle. A device that is offline tries nothing and notes nothing; `reconcilePushSubscription` and `resumePushSubscription` both answering `false` (notifications off here, and nothing to give back) sent nothing and note nothing. The comparison of endpoints reads the browser (`getActivePushSubscription`), never the database.
- **A device's push subscription belongs to the account that is signed in on it, and `claim_push_subscription` is what writes that down.** A browser holds ONE subscription for the app, whoever is signed in, and `push_subscriptions` holds one row per endpoint. [`lib/pushNotifications.js`](../src/lib/pushNotifications.js) never inserts or updates the table: `enablePush` (the toggle), `reconcilePushSubscription` and `resumePushSubscription` (the look of [`usePushAutoHeal`](../src/hooks/usePushAutoHeal.js)) all register the device with `supabase.rpc('claim_push_subscription', { p_endpoint, p_p256dh, p_auth, p_user_agent })` — no user id is sent, the function asks who calls. A subscription the previous user of the device left behind (a session that went away, a sign-out that could not release it, an app deployed before a sign-out released anything) therefore moves to the account that is signed in now, at its first look: that account receives here and the other one stops. Do not go back to an upsert: under the owner-only policies it is refused for the second account of a shared device, which is review P15 — that account could never switch notifications on, and the phone went on showing the first account's message previews. The app still reads (`isPushEnabled`) and deletes (`disablePush`, `releasePushSubscription`) its own rows directly, under those policies. `endpoint_keys_mismatch` — another account holds the address under keys that are not this browser's — is repaired only by `enablePush`, on the user's tap: the browser gives the address up and takes a new one, once. A look never does that.
- **A sign-out releases the device before the session ends, and only this device.** `signOut` of [`hooks/useAuth.jsx`](../src/hooks/useAuth.jsx) calls `releasePushSubscription({ userId })` after the guard has said yes and BEFORE `supabase.auth.signOut()`: the row of the browser's own subscription is deleted (`delete().eq('endpoint', …)` — found by the address of this device, never by the account: `eq('user_id', …)` would switch every phone of the account off) and the browser gives the subscription up (`unsubscribe()`). The order is the rule: the row is the account's own and only its session can delete it, so auth-js is asked once the delete has been answered. The step is best-effort and bounded, and it never throws — a sign-out is never stopped by it. The two halves go at the same time and either is enough (without the row nothing is sent here; without the subscription the push service answers 410 and send-push drops the row); offline the row is not asked for and the browser alone gives the subscription up; a request that hangs holds the sign-out for `RELEASE_TIMEOUT_MS` (4 s) at most. It reads the registration with `navigator.serviceWorker.getRegistration()`, not `ready`, which never settles in a browser where no worker took control. A session that goes away WITHOUT a sign-out (a refused refresh, a sign-out elsewhere) releases nothing: the same account is expected back, and another one takes the subscription over. `src/hooks/useAuth.push.test.jsx` holds the order and each way the release can fail, on the real provider and the real library.
- **Notifications come back with the account that had them on — and a look makes a subscription for nobody else.** Releasing the device at sign-out would by itself switch notifications off, without a word, for an athlete who signs out and in again and for two accounts that share a phone, at every switch. So `releasePushSubscription` writes the account down when it finds a subscription to release (`localStorage['sl_push_resume']`: user ids, newest last, eight at most — ids only), and when that account signs in on the device again the look of `usePushAutoHeal` calls `resumePushSubscription`: the browser takes a subscription and it is claimed. The list is what keeps the old rule true in its new form: **only the toggle and a resume ask the browser for a subscription**, and a resume only for an account on the list, only while `Notification.permission` is still `granted` (nothing is prompted), only when the device holds no subscription. An account leaves the list when the device is registered for it again (`enablePush`, `reconcilePushSubscription`, `resumePushSubscription`), when it switches notifications off (`disablePush`), and when the browser wants the user's own tap for a subscription (`NotAllowedError`, noted as `permission_denied`) — the toggle is the way then, and the look does not ask again. Whether iOS grants a subscription outside a tap, with the permission already given, is not something the test suite can say: on a device where it does not, the account finds its toggle off after signing back in, as it would without this.
- **What stops a notification toggle is a code of `lib/pushNotifications.js`, and the toggle prints `errorKey`.** Every failure of `enablePush`, `disablePush`, `isPushEnabled`, `reconcilePushSubscription` and `resumePushSubscription` is thrown as an `Error` with a `.code`: `unsupported`, `not_signed_in` (also a request that left without a session: `42501`), `permission_denied`, `sw_not_ready`, `offline` (the request got no answer — `isNetworkError` — or the device has no network, in which case nothing is sent and, in `enablePush`, the permission is not even asked for), `endpoint_refused` (`23514` naming `push_subscriptions_endpoint_known_push_service`: the browser's push service is not one the database accepts), `keys_mismatch`, `generic`. `PUSH_ERROR_KEYS` names the sentence of each code and `pushErrorKey(code)` answers `common.pushError.generic` for anything that is not in it. [`usePushSubscription`](../src/hooks/usePushSubscription.js) returns `errorKey` next to `errorCode`, and a toggle renders `t(errorKey)` — never `t('common.pushError.' + errorCode)`, which printed the key itself for an SQLSTATE or the number of a `DOMException`. A new code gets an entry in `PUSH_ERROR_KEYS` and its sentence in the three languages; `src/lib/pushNotifications.test.js` fails on a key the dictionaries do not have. Two things the hook does with a failure: a switch-off that removed neither the row nor the browser's subscription leaves the toggle ON (the device still receives), and a toggle that cannot ask the database whether the device is registered shows what the device itself last wrote (`lastHeal(userId).ok`) with the `offline` sentence, not a plain "off". When it opens, it also prints a failure of the background look that does not go away by itself (`LASTING`: `endpoint_refused`).

## App updates (a new version waits for the page)

- **A new service worker installs and WAITS; it takes over when a page tells it to.** `handleInstall` in [src/lib/swHandlers.js](../src/lib/swHandlers.js) calls `skipWaiting()` in one case only (next bullet); otherwise the worker stays behind the one that serves the app until a page posts `{ type: 'SKIP_WAITING' }` (`handleMessage`). Never put a bare `self.skipWaiting()` back in the `install` listener of [src/sw.js](../src/sw.js): the pages reload when their worker changes, and that reload would land in the middle of a rest or of a message being typed — which is what this flow exists to prevent. `src/sw.test.js` loads the real `sw.js` and fails on it; `src/lib/swHandlers.test.js` holds the handlers.
- **One worker still takes over by itself: the first of this kind on a device. A mark in Cache Storage tells it apart, and the mark must stay.** The client published before this flow registered its worker in "auto update" mode: its pages reload when a new worker activates and never send a message, so a worker that waited for them would wait until every window of the app is closed — and a reload is not a close. `handleActivate` therefore leaves one entry, `UPDATE_MARK_URL`, in a cache of its own, `UPDATE_MARK_CACHE` (`sl-app-update`); `handleInstall` reads it (`pagesAskForUpdates`): no mark means the pages on this device cannot ask, and the worker takes over at once, as every worker before it did. An unreadable Cache Storage is read as "no mark". Do not rename that cache to anything containing `-precache-` (workbox's `cleanupOutdatedCaches` deletes those), and do not clear it at sign-out. **What the mark cannot know is a rollback:** the app of a commit from before this flow, published again, takes over by itself and leaves the mark where it is — its pages cannot ask, and the worker of the deploy after it waits. On those devices that deploy arrives when the app is closed and opened again, not on a reload.
- **The page registers the worker itself. Nothing imports `virtual:pwa-register`, and `injectRegister` is `false` in [vite.config.js](../vite.config.js).** [src/main.jsx](../src/main.jsx) calls `appUpdater.start({ swUrl, scope })` (production builds only). The plugin's helper reloads every open window the moment a new worker activates, whatever the window is doing; and with `injectRegister` left at its default while nothing imports the virtual module, the plugin writes a `registerSW.js` into `index.html` — a second registration, with that same reload. `registerType: 'prompt'` changes nothing in an `injectManifest` build; it is there to say what the worker does. `src/main.test.jsx` scans `src/` for the import and reads the two options off the config.
- **The update flow has ONE `location.reload()`: `doReload` in [src/lib/appUpdate.js](../src/lib/appUpdate.js).** A worker that changes under a page that did not ask for it — another window applied the update, the first-worker case above, a rollback — does not reload that page: the page becomes `pending` like for any other update and reloads at a safe moment of its own, with no worker left to tell. A reload cannot loop: `AUTO_APPLIED_KEY` in `sessionStorage` holds the `BUILD_ID` of the build that last reloaded ITSELF, and a page that finds its own id there does not try a second time on its own (the offer still works; a tap does not use the attempt up). Where `sessionStorage` cannot be written, nothing is applied automatically.
- **The reload is asked for when the new worker is `activated` — never at `controllerchange`.** A worker that is told to take over becomes `activating` and the page hears `controllerchange` at once; the browser runs the worker's `activate` handlers only after that, and Chromium takes a full second to get there (1004 to 1015 ms on Chromium 147 with the handlers of `swHandlers.js`, which themselves need a few milliseconds). A reload asked for in that second is not refused: it is held back until `activated`, and the page it is going to replace stays on the screen meanwhile, takes taps and sends writes — which are then reloaded over. So `apply()` only remembers the worker it waits for (`awaited`: the one it told, or, for a page whose worker changed under it, `container.controller`), and `onWorkerState` calls `arrive()` when that worker's state is `activated`: 12 to 17 ms from there to the new page. `onControllerChange` reloads nothing; it marks the page `stale`. `pending` stays true for the whole of an attempt, so the card says "Updating…" until the page is replaced. An attempt whose worker is not `activated` after `APPLY_TIMEOUT_MS`, or whose worker becomes `redundant`, ends without a reload (`giveUp`) and the update is offered again. `fakeBrowser()` in [src/test/fakeServiceWorker.js](../src/test/fakeServiceWorker.js) keeps the browser's order — `activating(worker)` fires `controllerchange`, `activated(worker)` comes after, `takeOver` is the two — and a test of this flow that only calls `takeOver` cannot see the second in between.
- **"Safe" is five conditions, asked when the update is decided AND once more when the worker is `activated`, right before the reload.** The question is the probe `UpdatePrompt` registers with `appUpdater.watchForSafeMoment`: a reload would lose something while `queryClient.isMutating() > 0` (a write on its way or queued offline), while the rest timer's `endsAt` is in the future, while a field has the focus (`isField` in [src/lib/fieldWatch.js](../src/lib/fieldWatch.js): `textarea`, `select`, a typed `input`, an editable region — not a button or a checkbox), while a field holds text that was typed into it (`fieldWatch.holdsTypedText`, next bullet) or while a dialog is open (`dialog[open]`, `[role="dialog"][aria-modal="true"]`). The probe reads all five at the moment it is called, never from the last render. The second asking is in `arrive()`: the second the worker needs to be activated is a second in which a set can be ticked or a message begun, and that is exactly what the reload must not land on — the page then stays, already served by the new worker (`stale`), and reloads at the next safe moment. It applies to an update the page applied by itself; a tap on "Update" is the person's choice and is not asked again. **A new surface that holds unsaved input and is neither a field nor a dialog of those two shapes must add itself to the probe**, or an update reloads over it. **What the probe cannot see: a request that is not a React Query mutation** — `updateProfile` and `updatePassword` in [src/hooks/useAuth.jsx](../src/hooks/useAuth.jsx) are plain awaits. The password form sits in a dialog, which the probe sees; the rename of `student/StudentProfile` is sent from a `ui/EditableText` whose field is removed at once, and is on its way with nothing in the page to show it. A write that must not be reloaded over goes through `useMutation`.
- **Text that was typed and not sent is in progress wherever the focus is.** The drafts of `messaging/MessageComposer`, the exercise and goal forms and the fields of a set's sheet (`student/SetSheet`) live in `useState` and nowhere else: a reload at the next return to the app, with the focus on a button, would lose them. The feedback of `coach/SessionFeedbackComposer` is also kept on the device (`sl_feedback_draft:<sessionId>`, see "Coach session review") and comes back after a reload, but it counts here like the others: a reload still takes the coach off the page they were writing on. `fieldWatch` (listening to `input` and `change` on the document, in the capture phase, while `UpdatePrompt` is mounted) remembers the fields that were typed into; `holdsTypedText()` is true while one of them is still in the page (`isConnected`) with text that is not blank. A field that is emptied (a message that was sent) or removed (a form that closed, another page) no longer counts; a `select` never does. **It cannot tell sent from unsent**: a field that saves when it is left and keeps showing what was typed (a slot's sets and reps in the session editor, the roster's search box) counts until its page is left. That is the side to err on — the update is offered where it could have been applied — and the reason the probe must not be narrowed to forms or to `textarea`s: the fields of a set's sheet are neither.
- **The offer is not drawn while a field is in use, nor for `FIELD_LEFT_MS` after one is left.** `fieldWatch.inUse()` is "a field has the focus, or lost it less than 400 ms ago", and `UpdatePrompt` draws the offer only when it is false. The first half keeps the card off what is being typed, right above the keyboard. The second is about the tap that takes the focus away: a tap on a button moves the focus at the press and is a click only at the release, so a card drawn when the focus moves appears under the finger and takes the release — the click then belongs to neither element and the tap is lost: on a phone with its keyboard open, the "Create" of the exercise form sits where the card goes, and would send nothing. `src/components/ui/UpdatePrompt.test.jsx` holds the order of that tap. "Updating…" is not held back: it has no button. `inUse()` is about where the card is drawn and is NOT part of the probe — a tap that leaves the roster's search box for an athlete is a change of page, and a reload there costs nothing.
- **An update is considered at four moments, and "it became safe" is not one of them.** `consider()` runs when the update appears (a worker has installed and waits, or the worker changed under the page), when the app returns to the foreground, when the route changes, and when the shell mounts. The end of a rest, leaving a field and sending the message that was being typed change nothing by themselves — the next set is about to be ticked — so an update offered during a rest is applied at the next return to the app or the next page, or by a tap. A window that is not visible neither applies nor offers. The second asking of the bullet above is not a fifth moment: it can only call an attempt off.
- **A build below the oldest one still allowed is the one exception: its update goes in as soon as nothing would be lost.** The start of the updater and every question to the server (`check()` in [src/lib/appUpdate.js](../src/lib/appUpdate.js): the return to the app, `online`, the hourly tick) also read `version.json` (`readMinimum` → `fetchVersion` in [src/lib/buildInfo.js](../src/lib/buildInfo.js)). A page whose `BUILD_ID` is below the file's `minBuild` is `required` to update, and two things change — nothing else. `CHECK_MIN_GAP_MS` no longer holds its question back while it has no update (`mustAsk`): it asks for the new worker at every return to the app. And the probe is asked again every `REQUIRED_RETRY_MS` (`insist`): the end of a rest, a message that was sent, a dialog that closed are followed by the update within five seconds, where any other update waits for the next of the four moments. `insist` never offers (`consider({ offer: false })`), so "Later" keeps the card away until the next moment. Everything else is the ordinary flow: the same `AUTO_APPLIED_KEY` against a loop, no second attempt by the page after one that failed, the second asking in `arrive()`, nothing applied in a hidden window or on a page without a shell. `required` is not part of the state a component reads, and the card says nothing different. A read that fails — offline, not found, a page instead of the file — changes nothing: what was known stays known. **A page no service worker is registered for reads no `version.json`**: `start()` returns before it, and there is nothing to apply.
- **`version.json` is what the SERVER publishes now: never precached, and written by the build that wrote the id.** The `sl-build-id` plugin (`buildInfoPlugin`) emits it next to `index.html` — `{ "build": "<id>", "minBuild": "<id>" | null }` — from the id it writes into `<meta name="sl-build">`: one `makeBuildId` per build, so the two cannot differ, and `scripts/prune-gh-pages.mjs` refuses a `dist/` in which they do. `globPatterns` in `vite.config.js` has no `json`, on purpose: a copy in the precache would answer with what the worker was built with, which is the one thing the page already knows. Adding `json` to that list, an `additionalManifestEntries` entry or a runtime cache for the file breaks the minimum; `src/test/ciGuardrails.test.js` reads the pattern. The page asks with `cache: 'no-store'`; the host can still answer from its edge with the previous file for some ten minutes after a deploy.
- **`minBuild` is a time floor, set by the repository variable `MIN_CLIENT_BUILD` and written at build time.** A build id is the UTC time of its build (`YYYYMMDDHHmmss`), ids compare as strings, and "below" is `BUILD_ID < minBuild` (`isBelowMinimum`): the minimum is any time — the id of a build, or a moment between two builds — and every build made before it must update. `makeVersion` refuses a value that is not 14 digits — `2026-10-20` would pass for an id and compare as smaller than every build of the year, a minimum that does nothing — and a value later than the build itself: the page of that very build would be told for ever to update to a version that does not exist. The `deploy` job is then red at its build step and nothing is published. The variable takes effect with the next deploy and is written into every `version.json` after it, until it is changed or deleted. `NO_BUILD` is never below anything. An older app that is put back is a NEW build with a new id, so a minimum never keeps an old commit from being published again.
- **`UpdatePrompt` is mounted in `AppShell` only; a signed-out page has nobody to answer and its update waits.** It is applied when the shell mounts after sign-in, or when the app is closed and opened again. A reload of the login page does not bring it: a waiting worker waits for every window.
- **"Update" waits for the writes a reload would lose, for a bounded time.** `writesAreSafe` in [src/lib/pendingWrites.js](../src/lib/pendingWrites.js), awaited by `onUpdate` in [components/ui/UpdatePrompt.jsx](../src/components/ui/UpdatePrompt.jsx), waits for the writes that are on their way — up to `WRITES_WAIT_MS`, after which the offer comes back with `appUpdate.stillSaving` and nothing is reloaded — and then, if writes sit in the offline queue, for `QUEUE_ON_DISK_MS`, which must stay above the persister's `throttleTime` in [lib/queryClient.js](../src/lib/queryClient.js): a write queued this instant is in IndexedDB only once the throttle has passed. Raising the throttle means raising the constant.
- **The build id lives in `index.html`, never in a bundle.** The `sl-build-id` plugin — `buildInfoPlugin` in [src/lib/buildInfo.js](../src/lib/buildInfo.js), loaded by `vite.config.js` — writes `<meta name="sl-build" content="…">`; `BUILD_ID` in [src/lib/buildInfo.js](../src/lib/buildInfo.js) reads it back (`NO_BUILD` where no build wrote one). A constant that changes with every build, inlined into the entry chunk, renames that chunk and every chunk that imports it: a deploy that changed no source would be a full download on every device. An id is the UTC time of the build, `YYYYMMDDHHmmss`, so ids compare as strings in the order of the builds; `SL_BUILD_ID` in the environment replaces it and must sort the same way (`makeBuildId` refuses anything but 1 to 64 letters, digits, `_`, `.`, `-`). **Consequence: every build has a new `index.html`, so a new `sw.js` — every deploy is an update for every device, including a deploy that changed no source.**
- **The page asks for a new version itself, and not more often than `CHECK_MIN_GAP_MS`.** A hash route change is not a navigation, so the browser never asks for a tab that stays open: `appUpdate` calls `registration.update()` on the return to the foreground, on `online`, and every `CHECK_EVERY_MS` (one hour). The gap (15 minutes) is what keeps the unlock between two sets from costing a request each time; a question that got no answer (no network) does not count towards it.

## A page older than the files the server keeps

- **A file of the app that did not load is not a crash.** `isChunkLoadError` in [src/lib/chunkRecovery.js](../src/lib/chunkRecovery.js) is the one definition — by the browsers' wordings (`NOT_LOADED`), because the type is a `TypeError` here and a plain `Error` there; a request to the API that failed ("Failed to fetch") is not one. Three places go by it: the `vite:preloadError` listener of the same file, `ErrorBoundary` (`componentDidCatch` reports nothing — neither the error nor the component stack — and the screen says "A new version of the app is available" with one action, "Reload"), and the `unhandledrejection` handler of [src/lib/errorReporter.js](../src/lib/errorReporter.js) (a screen fetched ahead of its use by `lib/preload` is awaited by nobody). A wording a browser starts to use goes into `NOT_LOADED` with a row in `src/lib/chunkRecovery.test.js`.
- **Never "Try again" for it.** `React.lazy` keeps a rejected import for good: re-rendering the same tree fails on the same file. The way on is a reload, which brings the page of the build that is published.
- **The page reloads by itself for it once per build id, online, and only when the update flow's probe says nothing would be lost.** `recover()` in `chunkRecovery.js`, called for every `vite:preloadError` (installed by [src/main.jsx](../src/main.jsx), production builds only). `CHUNK_RELOADED_KEY` in `sessionStorage` holds the `BUILD_ID` of the build that reloaded itself: a page that finds its own id there is the same build again and does not try a second time, and where the guard cannot be written nothing reloads. It is a key of its own, not `AUTO_APPLIED_KEY` — the two reloads have different causes, and at worst a build reloads once for each. Offline the same error means "no network", and a page no worker serves would be reloaded into the browser's own error page. The probe is `appUpdater.busy()`: `true`, `false`, or `null` while no shell is mounted, which counts as "no" — nothing of what the probe is about can be in progress then. When any of this says no, the error goes on to `React.lazy` and to the boundary, where the person decides.
- **The `vite:preloadError` event is never cancelled.** `preventDefault()` makes Vite swallow the error, and the import then resolves to `undefined`: `React.lazy` fails a second time, on "Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'default')" — which `isChunkLoadError` does not know, and which would be shown and reported as a crash.
- **This reload is not the update flow's, and does not wait for a worker.** It is a plain `location.reload()`: it asks the worker that serves the page now — or the server, when none does — for the page again. A page whose worker changed under it (`stale` in `appUpdate.js`) is the update flow's business first: the change of route that asks for the missing file is a moment, and `consider()` reloads the page there, online or not, when nothing would be lost. Chunk recovery is for the page the update flow knows nothing about: one that no worker serves, or whose worker lost its cache.
- **"Reload" on that screen waits for the writes a reload would drop, then reloads whatever happened.** `handleReload` in [components/ui/ErrorBoundary.jsx](../src/components/ui/ErrorBoundary.jsx) awaits `writesAreSafe` ([src/lib/pendingWrites.js](../src/lib/pendingWrites.js)) — the wait "Update" has — and then calls `recovery.reloadNow()` even when a write did not come back within `WRITES_WAIT_MS`: unlike the update card, this screen has replaced the whole app and offers nothing else to do. The query client is read through `static contextType = QueryClientContext`, not a hook, so the boundary still draws where no provider is above it. While the device is offline the screen does not promise a new version: it says the screen could not be loaded, and to try the reload.
- **Whoever reloads the page asks `appUpdater.busy()` first, or leaves it to a tap.** The app has three reloads: `doReload` in `appUpdate.js` (the update flow's one), `reload` in `chunkRecovery.js`, and the buttons of `ErrorBoundary` ("Reload", "Go home"), which are taps. A fourth follows the same rule.
- **A deploy publishes the new build ON TOP of what is published, and the files of the last three builds stay.** A page that was opened before a deploy asks for the files of ITS build when a screen is opened for the first time. The `deploy` job of `.github/workflows/deploy.yml` therefore copies the `gh-pages` branch into `published/` (step "What is published now": `git archive`, so no `.git` comes with it), runs `node scripts/prune-gh-pages.mjs --site published --dist dist`, and hands `published/` to the action. **`publish_dir` is `./published`, never `./dist`** — `dist/` alone is the old behaviour, in which every deploy took the files of every other build away. **No `keep_files`**: the action replaces the branch with the folder it is given, which already holds what stays; with `keep_files` nothing the script deleted would ever leave the branch. `src/test/ciGuardrails.test.js` holds the order of the steps, the folder, and runs the first step against a real repository.
- **The prune deletes only files under `assets/` that a record names, and stops rather than guess.** Each build leaves `builds/<build id>.json` — `{ "build", "files" }`, its files under `assets/`. `planSite` in [scripts/lib/published-builds.mjs](../scripts/lib/published-builds.mjs) keeps the `KEEP_BUILDS` (3) records with the latest ids, this build included and always kept, and deletes the others with every file they name that no kept record names — a file that did not change between two builds has the same name in both. Nothing outside `assets/` and `builds/` is ever deleted: `index.html`, `sw.js`, `version.json`, the manifest and the icons are overwritten by each build, and a file put on the branch by hand stays. A file under `assets/` that no record names stays too, and the run says so. A record that cannot be read or says it is another build, a record that names a path outside `assets/` (`..`, an absolute path, `index.html`), anything else in `builds/`, a link under `assets/`, a `dist/` whose `index.html` and `version.json` are not of the same build: `SiteError`, exit 1, **nothing written** — the job stops before the publish and the app that was live stays.
- **A published tree without records is adopted, not emptied.** That is the site as it was before wave 2, and the site after an emergency publish. Its files under `assets/` become the record of the build `ADOPTED_BUILD` (`00000000000000`, older than every id), so the pages that still run it keep their files until three newer builds exist. Adoption happens only when there is no record at all.
- **The files of a build live under `assets/`, and the script is told nowhere else.** `ASSETS_DIR` in `published-builds.mjs` is Vite's default `build.assetsDir`. Setting `assetsDir` in `vite.config.js` means changing it there in the same commit; the guardrails test fails on an `assetsDir` in the config.
- **One publish at a time, and a branch that could not be read stops the job.** The `deploy` job reads the branch, adds to it and writes it back: its `concurrency` group (`publish-site`, `cancel-in-progress: false`) keeps two runs from each publishing a tree without the other's build. The step that reads the branch tells "there is no `gh-pages` branch" (`git ls-remote --exit-code` ends with 2: an empty folder, a first deploy) from "it could not be read" (any other status: exit 1) — published as if nothing were there, the build would go out alone.
- **The emergency publish keeps nothing.** `npm run deploy` is `gh-pages -d dist`: it replaces the branch with the build of the owner's machine. The files of the builds before it and the records are gone; a page that was open since before reloads by itself at the next screen it opens for the first time, or shows the "Reload" screen. The deploy after it adopts what it finds.

## RLS / DB conventions

- **RLS is strict.** New tables need both student- and coach-side policies walking session → profile via `student_profile_for_session()` / `coach_profile_for_session()` helpers in `schema.sql`. A helper a policy calls must be executable by `authenticated`: a new one joins the `FUNCTION PRIVILEGES` block at the end of `schema.sql` and the `HELPERS` list of `supabase/tests/function-acl.test.js`. A database change is a migration in the format of `supabase/migrations/README.md` *and* the same change merged into `schema.sql`. A new table enables row-level security in the migration that creates it and is defined above the `TABLE PRIVILEGES` block of `schema.sql`; `supabase/tests/table-acl.test.js` fails on one that has none.
- **A coach-wide read names the coach; RLS is not the tenant filter.** RLS answers "what may this account read", and an account may read the rows it coaches *and* the rows in which it is the athlete: `students` has both SELECT policies, the athlete's own `programs` and `session_confirmations` follow, and `exercise_library` is readable by the athletes of its owner. For a coach who is also somebody's athlete — an account promoted to coach that keeps its `students` row, or a forged link — the two sets differ, and an unfiltered read puts that coach in their own roster with their own block, and another coach's exercises in their pickers. So [`useStudents`](../src/hooks/useStudents.js) filters `coach_id = user.id`; [`useCoachDashboardPrograms`](../src/hooks/useProgram.js) and the coach's confirmation reads of [`useSessionConfirmation.js`](../src/hooks/useSessionConfirmation.js) (one builder, `coachConfirmations`, behind `useReviewInbox`, `useConfirmationTotals` and `useConfirmationHistory`) embed `student:students!inner(coach_id)` and filter on it; `coach_review_counts()`, which `useReviewCounts` asks first, filters the same way in the database; [`useExerciseLibrary`](../src/hooks/useExerciseLibrary.js) filters `coach_id` when the role is coach. Two things to keep: **a filter on an embedded row only drops the parent row when every embed on its path is `!inner`** — remove one `!inner` and the request still answers 200, with the filter doing nothing, which is why the hook tests assert the select as well as the filter — and **a new read across all of a coach's athletes needs the same filter**. A read that is given one athlete's id is scoped by that id and does not. `useStudents` returns an empty list to an athlete, which is right: `MessageThread` calls it for both roles and reads it only as a coach.
