The classification reads the *shape* of the error, never its message: Safari words a failed fetch in the language of the phone. The client-wide default stays `retry: 0` and must: a write outside the queue is usually a bare INSERT, and re-sent after an answer got lost it is a second row. Anything added to `MUTATION_FNS` must be idempotent for the same reason.
- **The pages wait for the replay of the queue while it moves, and only then.** `PersistQueryClientProvider` lets no query fetch until the promise of its `onSuccess` settles. That is wanted: a read that came back before the queued writes reached the database would replace the ticked sets on screen with the rows as they were, and nothing refreshes a cache after a replayed write (a hydrated mutation has no hook around it). It is fatal if unbounded: the promise of `resumeMutationsAndReconcileDrafts` settles when the replay has — after the retries of a write, never while one waits for a session — and every page would sit on its spinner behind one queued set. `App.jsx` therefore passes `replayQueuedWrites` (`lib/queryClient.js`), whose promise settles when the queue has been replayed **or** as soon as the replay has to wait: the device is offline, an attempt failed and will be made again, or nothing has settled for `REPLAY_PATIENCE_MS` (5 s: a request that hangs, a token refresh that does). Never hand the provider the replay's own promise, and never hand it nothing. React Query applies the same order on its own on `online` and on focus (`await resumePausedMutations()`, then the refetch), without the limit: while a resumed write waits, that refetch waits with it.
- **A set is dated at the tap: `logged_at` and `failed_at` are never read from the clock of the request.** A set ticked without signal is sent when the phone finds a network again — the evening, or days later after a relaunch — and `performed_on` (the earliest `logged_at` of the session), "N days ago", the recent records and the bodyweight of a ×BW ratio all follow that timestamp. So the moment travels with the write: a set-log hook puts `at` (ISO) in the mutation variables, where its optimistic patch reads it too, and `patchForDone(done, at)` / `patchForFailed(failed, at)` in [`lib/offlineMutations.js`](../src/lib/offlineMutations.js) build the patch from it. The variables are what the persister stores. A write that carries no `at` (queued by an older build, or by a hook that does not send it) is dated by `state.submittedAt` of its mutation — React Query sets it on the tap and it is persisted with the queue — which `queuedWrite` hands to the function as `madeAt`; only a call that knows neither reads the clock. A value that is not a date is skipped, not sent. Whoever adds a write that stamps a moment takes it from the variables the same way (`tapTime`); `new Date()` inside a function of `MUTATION_FNS` is the bug this bullet is about.
- **The reads a workout write makes stale are refreshed by the defaults of its mutation key, not by the hook.** A hook exists while its page does; a workout logged offline is usually replayed after the app was killed and opened again, by mutations that have nothing but the defaults `registerOfflineMutationDefaults` registered. `refreshAfter` is their `onSettled`: confirm and undo invalidate `session-confirmation`, `my-confirmed-session-ids`, `week-confirmed-session-ids`, `program-confirmed-session-ids`, `student-progress-stats`, `student-records` and `student-lifetime-stats`; a note or a deviation invalidates `['slot-comments', sessionId]` / `['slot-deviations', sessionId]` (the whole root when the variables name no session — `[root, undefined]` matches nothing); a set-log write invalidates `['set-logs']` once, when no other `set-log` write is left unsettled. That last test is not `isMutating(...) === 1`: sets restored after a relaunch leave in parallel (see the "Replay ordering" sentence replaced below), and when two answers arrive in the same turn each write still sees the other as pending — so `settling` counts out the writes already in their `onSettled`. Three things follow. (1) A hook on one of these keys adds `onMutate` and `onError` (the optimistic update and its rollback) and **no `onSettled`**: an option set by the hook REPLACES the default of the same name, it does not run next to it, and its list would be the one that silently stops matching. (2) A new read that a confirmation changes is added to `CONFIRMATION_READS`, not to a hook. (3) The refresh is started and never returned: `onSettled` keeps its mutation pending for as long as the promise it returns, and with it the next write of the FIFO scope.
- **A write that waited and is then refused because its session was closed gets one message, a journal entry and no refresh.** An athlete's write is accepted while their program is the block being trained (with 72 hours of grace after a block switch once migration 17, `replay_grace_window`, is applied) and the session is neither archived nor reviewed. Past that, every write of a workout logged offline comes back with `PGRST116` (an UPDATE read back with `.single()` that matched no writable row) or `42501` (an INSERT its policy turned down, or the trigger on undoing a reviewed confirmation) — `isClosedToAthlete` in [`lib/mutationErrors.js`](../src/lib/mutationErrors.js). `queuedWrite` treats such a failure apart when the write had WAITED: its mutation was restored from IndexedDB, was paused while the device was offline, or was kept waiting for a session (`waited`; a mutation that is "paused" behind another one of its scope on a working network has not waited). Then: the error is marked (`isRefusedReplay`), the client's `MutationCache.onError` in [`lib/queryClient.js`](../src/lib/queryClient.js) shows no toast for it, and `refuseReplay` pushes `offlineReplay.refused` ONCE per replay — from the first refusal until no workout write is pending; the write is appended to the journal; and `refreshAfter` is skipped until the queue is empty, so the mutation layer does not replace the workout on screen with the rows the database kept. A refusal of a write that had not waited, and any other code, stay ordinary failures (generic toast, the hook's rollback, the refresh). Draft writes are outside all of it: `draftSaveErrorHandler` has its own answer to `42501`.
- **`sl_rejected_writes` (localStorage) is the journal of refused replays, and the only copy of what they carried.** `recordRejectedWrite` appends `{ refused_at, made_at, user_id, write, variables, code, message }` — `write` is the name in `MUTATION_KEYS`, `variables` what the athlete did (set id, done or failed, RPE, actuals, note, tap time). Capped at `REJECTED_WRITES_CAP` (300) entries, oldest out first; reading and writing never throw. It is not part of the query cache: `queryClient.clear()` and the IndexedDB wipe leave it, so signing out does not destroy the only copy. Entries carry the `user_id` of the session that sent them: a reader shows a user only their own, and the code that handles a real change of account decides what becomes of the others (`clearRejectedWrites` empties the journal). Nothing in the app reads the journal yet.
- **A draft tree that holds edits the database does not have says so in its own data, and no read of the server replaces it.** The marker is `tree.unsynced = { rev }` on the `['draft-tree', programId]` entry ([`lib/draftTree.js`](../src/lib/draftTree.js): `markUnsynced`, `markSynced`, `isUnsyncedDraft`, `unsyncedRev`, `nextDraftRev`). It is part of the cached DATA on purpose: the persister stores the data, so the marker is still there after the app was killed — a save that failed is not, React Query only stores paused mutations. `useCreateDraft` and `commit` in [`hooks/useAuthoring.js`](../src/hooks/useAuthoring.js) write the edit and its marker in ONE `setQueryData` and put the same `rev` in the save's variables (`{ tree, rev }`); the `onSuccess` default of `saveDraftTree` (`draftSaved` in [`lib/offlineMutations.js`](../src/lib/offlineMutations.js)) removes the marker only when the tree still has that `rev`, so the answer to one save says nothing about an edit made after it. While the marker is there: the query functions of `useDraftTree` and `useMyDraft` answer from the cache — before asking the server and again when its answer arrives — so a refetch from any source (remount, focus, reconnect, `invalidateQueries`) makes no request and replaces nothing, and `my-draft` keeps the pointer of a draft the server has no row for yet; and `resumeMutationsAndReconcileDrafts` skips its invalidation (`hasUnsyncedDraft`: a marked tree, or a draft write — save or discard — still pending). Whoever adds a write to the draft tree marks it the same way, and whoever adds a read of `draft-tree` or `my-draft` outside those two hooks repeats their guard: an unguarded read puts the tree of before the edits back, or answers "no draft" for one created offline, and the next edit then saves that over the server. `buildSnapshot` (same file as the marker) copies field by field, so the marker never reaches `save_draft_tree`.
- **A marked tree is sent again until the database has it — on reconnect, at launch, when a session appears and on return to the app — and a tree in sync is read again on those same occasions.** `resumeMutationsAndReconcileDrafts` does both and decides between them: `sendUnsyncedDrafts` builds a save from every marked tree that no pending `saveDraftTree` mutation already carries (same key, same `draft-tree` scope and retry policy, variables `{ tree, rev, resend: true }`; offline it pauses, which is what the persister stores), then the reconcile invalidates `my-draft` and `draft-tree` only if nothing is unsynced. Its four callers: the persister's `onSuccess` and the auth listener (through `replayQueuedWrites`), the `online` listener of `AppShell`, and the `focusManager` listener of [`lib/queryClient.js`](../src/lib/queryClient.js) — the return to the app, which is also how a draft the coach sent back or approved shows up without a reload. What a failed save leaves is decided in `draftSaveErrorHandler` (a mutation default, so a save restored or re-sent without a hook behaves the same): **no answer** after the retries of the queue (`isTransientError`) → the tree stays marked, one toast, and the re-sends that follow by themselves are silent; **refused** (`exercise_unavailable`, or any other coded answer) → the tree stays marked, the toast is shown, and that `rev` is remembered in memory (`turnedDown`) so the same snapshot is not sent again by itself — the next edit makes a new `rev`, and a relaunch tries once more; **`draft_not_editable` / `23505` / `42501`** → the server won: the marker is dropped FIRST (it is what keeps the read out), then both roots are invalidated. A refused snapshot that carried a submission takes `submitted_at` back to NULL on the tree and on the `my-draft` pointer: the save is one transaction, so the database did not record it, and a submitted draft cannot be edited — it would stay locked on a tree the athlete has to change. A submission that got no answer is not taken back; it is re-sent, which is the only way it ever leaves, since a submitted draft has no "next edit".
- **`my-draft` and `draft-tree` are kept in the cache for 14 days (`PERSIST_MAX_AGE_MS`, the `gcTime` every persisted root gets from `setQueryDefaults` in `lib/queryClient.js`), not the default five minutes.** An entry nobody observes is collected after its `gcTime`, and what leaves the cache is written out of IndexedDB on the next persist: with the default a marked tree would be gone once the athlete has spent five minutes on another page, or has launched the app without opening the builder. Whatever prunes the persisted cache (by age, by root) must leave a marked tree alone — it has no other copy; `shouldPersistQuery` asks `holdsUnsyncedDraft` before either of its two rules, for the tree and for the `my-draft` pointer to it.
- **What the device has read is kept for 14 days, and one constant says so in three places.** `PERSIST_MAX_AGE_MS` in [`lib/queryClient.js`](../src/lib/queryClient.js) is the `maxAge` of the persisted cache (`App.jsx`), the `gcTime` of every root of `PERSISTED_QUERY_ROOTS` (one `setQueryDefaults` call per root — a second call for the same root REPLACES the first, so a root's refetch rules go into `REFETCH_RULES` and are merged there), and the age at which `shouldPersistQuery` stops storing one entry. They have to agree: an entry nobody observes is collected after its `gcTime`, and what leaves memory is written out of IndexedDB on the next save. With React Query's default of five minutes a session opened at home was off the device before the gym, and `my-confirmed-session-ids` — observed by the training list only — was gone five minutes into any workout, which made the list start again from the first session at the next launch without a network. A root added to the allow-list gets the `gcTime` by being in the list.
- **`shouldPersistQuery` is also the only thing that takes an entry OFF the device.** With a 14-day `gcTime` nothing is collected in an app that is opened every day (the timers start again at each launch), so an entry left out of a save is what "evicted" means: it is not there after the next launch, while the running app keeps it in memory. Two rules: an entry whose `dataUpdatedAt` is older than `PERSIST_MAX_AGE_MS`, and every entry of a session confirmed more than `CONFIRMED_SESSION_KEPT_MS` (7 days) ago — `session`, `set-logs`, `slot-comments`, `slot-deviations`, `session-confirmation`, `set-videos` and `last-performance`, whose keys carry the session id at the position `SESSION_ID_AT` gives. "Confirmed … ago" is read off the `confirmed_at` of the `['session-confirmation', sessionId]` entry in the cache; a session whose confirmation is not cached, or was undone (`null`), is not known to be finished and stays. A root that is keyed by session and joins the allow-list joins `SESSION_ID_AT`, or its entries of finished sessions pile up in a blob that is re-serialised on every change of the cache. One exception comes before both rules: a draft tree that carries the unsynced marker, and the `my-draft` pointer to it (`holdsUnsyncedDraft`), are stored whatever their age. What the rules cannot touch is the queue: a write that waits is a mutation, stored apart from the queries, so a session whose entries were left out still gets its queued sets and its confirmation sent. The function is also called by other suites with a bare `{ queryKey, state: { status: 'success' } }` to ask "is this key stored": without `data` and `dataUpdatedAt` it answers on the key alone, and has to keep doing so.
- **An entry that holds data is stored whatever became of its last fetch, and is stored as what was read.** A refresh that fails keeps the data and sets `status: 'error'`; `shouldPersistQuery` used to store `'success'` only, so one request lost in a basement took the session off the device. It now stores anything with data (a read that never succeeded has none). React Query restores a status as it finds it, and without a network an entry restored as `'error'` stays one — so `serializeCache`, the persister's `serialize`, rewrites such an entry (`asLastRead`: `status: 'success'`, `error` and the failure counters cleared) and keeps `isInvalidated`, which a failed fetch sets: the entry comes back as data that is out of date, and is read again as soon as it can be. A page may therefore treat `isError` on a persisted root as "this launch could not read it"; it never means "the stored copy is broken".
- **A return to the app re-reads five roots only when they are due — and the rule is not the same for all five.** Left to the client's defaults (stale after two minutes, refetch on focus and on reconnect) every unlock after a rest re-read the whole logging page, the athlete's entire set history included. `REFETCH_RULES` in `lib/queryClient.js` takes `session`, `slot-comments`, `slot-deviations`, `exercise-library` and `last-performance` out of that, on the strength of the invalidations the app's own writes already make. Rule 1, the first four (`QUIET_WHILE_OPEN`): `refetchOnWindowFocus` and `refetchOnReconnect` are the function `dueOnReturn` — true only for an entry the app flagged (`isInvalidated`: an invalidation that could not be served, a refresh that failed) or one older than `QUIET_MS` (4 hours, longer than any workout); their `staleTime` is NOT raised, so a page that opens on a copy older than two minutes still reads it again. Rule 2, the athlete's own hint (`['last-performance', 'self', …]`): `staleTime` is `QUIET_MS` (`hintStaleTime`), opening the page included; the hint a coach reads, keyed by the athlete, keeps two minutes. **Do not "simplify" rule 1 into a long `staleTime`.** These four keys are shared by both roles and written from another device — the coach edits a session the athlete holds, the athlete's notes and swaps are what the coach's review shows, the coach adds an exercise on a second device — nothing pushes those edits, and the cache is stored with its timestamps, so relaunching the app does not make a copy older: with a long `staleTime` an athlete would train on the session as it was before the coach's edit, with no way to get the new one. What rule 1 costs is the refresh a return to a page used to give: a coach's editor or review left open is brought up to date by reopening it (or by its own next write, which invalidates), not by coming back to the tab, until four hours have passed. Two things silently undo the rules: an option set in the hook (`useQuery({ staleTime })` wins over a root default — `useLastPerformance` sets none for that reason), and a read of one of these roots that relies on focus to stay current — `SessionView` asks for the session row explicitly at the moments a review may have happened for that reason (the bullet "**`reviewed_at` comes from a cached row, and nothing pushes a review to the athlete's device.**"). The request counts are in `src/lib/queryClient.cache.test.js` ("the phone is unlocked after a rest").
- **The device stores the writes of the queue — every one that has not settled — and no other mutation.** `isQueuedWrite` in [`lib/persistence.js`](../src/lib/persistence.js) is `state.status === 'pending'` and a `mutationKey` that is one of `MUTATION_KEYS`, part for part (`isQueueKey`); it is the `shouldDehydrateMutation` of `persistOptions`. Both halves replace the library's default, which stores whatever is *paused*. (1) **The key.** A mutation restored from IndexedDB is a key and its variables; its function comes from the defaults registered under that key. One stored without a key of the queue comes back with no function, fails with "No mutationFn found" and shows "Couldn't save that" — to an athlete who had only opened a session without signal, whose page paused a housekeeping write. (2) **Not settled, rather than paused.** A write is paused while the device is offline. On a network that does not answer it is not: its request is out for as long as the phone lets it hang, or it sleeps between two attempts, and while nobody is signed in it sleeps and looks again without limit. An app killed in one of those moments used to lose the write. Storing a write whose request may have reached the database is safe for one reason only: every function of `MUTATION_FNS` can be sent twice — which [`src/lib/offlineSafety.test.js`](../src/lib/offlineSafety.test.js) reads off the code (`WHAT_A_QUEUED_WRITE_SENDS`: an `update` of the row named by the variables, an `upsert` with `onConflict`, a `delete`, or the `save_draft_tree` RPC; never an `insert`). **A new queued write** therefore needs four things, not three: the key in `MUTATION_KEYS`, the function in `MUTATION_FNS`, its line in `WHAT_A_QUEUED_WRITE_SENDS`, and its key in the list pinned by `src/lib/persistence.test.js`.
- **A write is stored, and taken back, as one that waits.** `asWaiting` in `lib/persistence.js` runs on the way to the store (`persistClient`) and on the way back (`restoreClient`): `isPaused: true`, the failure count and its reason reset, and no `context`. `isPaused` is what `resumePausedMutations` looks for — a write restored as "pending, not paused" is never resumed and, being the first pending mutation of its scope, blocks every write behind it for good. `context` is the snapshot a hook took for its rollback (every cached list of sets, once per queued set): a restored write has no hook around it, so nothing can read it, and it was most of the blob the persister re-writes at each change of the cache. The live mutation is not touched — its hook still rolls back from its own `context`. Consequence for the mutation defaults in `lib/offlineMutations.js`: a handler registered there must not read `context` (the third argument of `onSuccess` / `onError` / `onSettled`); after a relaunch it is `undefined`. The restore also drops every stored mutation that is not a pending write of the queue, so a blob written by a build that stored more (the one published on 2026-10-13 stores every paused mutation) gives back only what can be replayed.
- **`CACHE_BUSTER` is the version of the stored READS. The queue goes through a change of it, and through the 14 days.** `persistQueryClientRestore` throws the whole blob away when its `buster` differs or it is older than `maxAge` — the mutation queue with it: the one documented way to handle a change of shape would have deleted the unsynced workout of every device it reached, and so did two weeks without opening the app. So `restoreClient` in `lib/persistence.js` makes both tests itself, on the reads alone, and hands the library a blob that always passes its own (current `buster`, fresh `timestamp`): a blob of another version, too old or without a date gives back **no read and every queued write**. Two kinds of read are kept with the queue, because no request can make them again: a `['draft-tree', id]` entry that carries the unsynced marker, and the `['my-draft', …]` entry that points to it. Three rules follow. (1) Change `CACHE_BUSTER` (in `lib/persistence.js`, nowhere else) when the shape of a persisted query changes in a way the pages cannot read; it costs every device one refetch and nothing else. (2) **A key of `MUTATION_KEYS` is never removed or renamed**: a device may hold a write made under it by any earlier build, the restore drops what has no registered key, and that is a workout thrown away without a word. `src/lib/persistence.test.js` pins the eleven keys. (3) **A write whose VARIABLES change shape gets a new key next to the old one**, and the old key keeps its old function for as long as a device may still hold a write made under it — the buster does not version the queue, and an old-shaped write is replayed by the function of today (a confirmation queued before `performedOn` existed is sent without its training date).
- **The persisted blob says whose it is, and a blob left by another account is wiped at launch.** The persister of `lib/persistence.js` writes `owner` (a user id, or `null`) next to `buster` and `timestamp`, reads it back at launch (`queryPersister.getOwner()`), and forgets it in `removeClient()`. A state is labelled with the owner of the moment it was TAKEN, not of the moment the throttled write lands — so what one account's late write leaves after a wipe is still under that account's name. `setOwner(userId)` only labels (and writes the label at once, without waiting for another change of the cache); it never relabels another account's data, which is why `ClearOnUserChange` in [`App.jsx`](../src/App.jsx) wipes first. That component wipes memory (`queryClient.clear()`) and device (`removeClient()`) in exactly three cases: **another account** is signed in than the owner of the cache, **the user signed out of this device** (`signOuts` of `useAuth` moved), or **the cache has no owner and nobody is signed in**. The owner is `cacheOwner.get()` of [`lib/queryClient.js`](../src/lib/queryClient.js): the persister's `getOwner()`, or a variable of that module on a browser without IndexedDB. It is one answer on purpose — the gate of the queue asks the same one (`queueOwner`), and it asks first: a sign-in replays the queue from the auth listener before `ClearOnUserChange` has run, so an owner that only the component knew held no write back, and one account's queued set left under the session of the next. "Nobody is signed in" is, for a cache that HAS an owner, none of the three: it is also what a session looks like that went away without anyone asking — a refresh the server refused, a sign-out on another device or in another tab — and what the device holds then stays, labelled with its owner, for the same account to find again; another account falls under the first case. Wiping on any change of the signed-in user deleted the unsynced workout on the reconnect that should have sent it (nobody → the same person, after an offline launch on an expired token). A cache WITHOUT an owner — a blob written by a build that did not label it (every device of the app as deployed at `main@fc7a552` holds one), or what was read since the last wipe — is taken for the account the device is signed in as when it is opened, and labelled. Opened by nobody it can be kept for no one, and it is wiped at that moment, not when the next account arrives: a write without a label is held back by nothing (`ownsQueue`), and it would leave under the newcomer's session on the replay their sign-in starts. The component decides nothing while auth is loading, and nothing while the blob is still being read (`useIsRestoring()`): a wipe then would be followed by the restore it was meant to prevent, so a sign-out seen in that moment — and the fact that nobody was signed in — is remembered and acted on once the restore is over. It labels the cache only with a KNOWN account. `src/App.test.jsx` holds the cases on the real client and persister, and `src/App.offline.test.jsx` the whole trace with the real auth provider around them: a launch offline on an expired token with a queued set, the network back, the set sent once and nothing wiped — and, for a blob without a label and for a browser without IndexedDB, another account's sign-in with the replay running before React renders, which is the order of a browser (the sign-in and a pause inside ONE `act` scope: a test that lets the scope close first has the wipe win every time and proves nothing about the gate).
- **A queued write is sent under the session of the account its queue belongs to, and under no other.** The wipe above comes late for one thing: the provider replays the restored queue from its `onSuccess`, before any component can look at the owner, and a write asleep between two attempts wakes up whenever its delay is over. Sent under another account's session such a write is refused by RLS — taken off the queue, and reported to the wrong person. So `lib/queryClient.js` registers the queue with `registerOfflineMutationDefaults(queryClient, { queueOwner })`, where `queueOwner` is `cacheOwner.get` — the persister's owner, or the one `lib/queryClient.js` keeps itself on a browser without IndexedDB, where the queue lives in memory and its account's session can go away under it — and `ownsQueue` in [`lib/offlineMutations.js`](../src/lib/offlineMutations.js) is asked in the two places that already ask for a session: `resumeMutationsAndReconcileDrafts` replays and reconciles nothing, and `queuedWrite` throws `AuthNotReadyError` — the write waits, exactly as it does while nobody is signed in — until the wipe removes it or its owner is back. An owner that is not known (`null`: nothing restored, a blob written before the label, a client registered without `queueOwner`) holds nothing back — which is why `ClearOnUserChange` never lets a cache without an owner wait for a sign-in: opened by a signed-in account it is labelled, opened by nobody it is wiped at once.
- **The cache is saved once at the moment the provider starts saving, by the LAST child of the provider.** `PersistQueryClientProvider` subscribes to the caches only when the restore is over — and the restore includes the promise of `onSuccess`, the replay of the queue, which is waited for while it moves (seconds on a slow network, with the restored pages on screen). What changes before that is in memory only: a queued write that reached the database is still stored as owed (and is sent a second time if the app is killed), and a set ticked meanwhile is not stored at all, until something else happens to change the cache. `SaveWhenRestored` in `App.jsx` calls `persistQueryClientSave` when `useIsRestoring()` turns false. Its position is load-bearing: React runs the effects of a commit children first and siblings in order, so as the last child of the provider its effect runs after those of every page and of `ClearOnUserChange` (a wipe decided in that pass is saved as the emptied cache, not followed by a save of what was wiped; a write a page makes as it mounts is in the save) and immediately before the provider's own, which subscribes — nothing can happen in between. `CacheBoundary` passes `persistOptions` whole; rebuilding it option by option in `App.jsx` is how the persister and the filter that goes with it come apart.
- **After a relaunch each scope replays in the order its writes were made — tested through the store.** `src/lib/persistence.test.js` drives the real hooks without a network, saves with the app's `persistOptions` (the library's `persistQueryClientSave`), reads the string in the store, restores it into a freshly evaluated app (`persistQueryClientRestore`) and looks at the requests in the order they leave: two taps on one set stay in order, the sets of two rows do not wait for each other, confirm then undo then a note leave one by one, the snapshots of a draft stay in order on a scope that holds no set back, and a set is dated at the tap. A test that hands `dehydrate()` output straight to `hydrate()` skips the three places where this goes wrong — the filter, the stored shape and the restore rules.
- **Each step of a set-log write touches one row, and of that row the fields it changed.** The athlete ticks the next set while the last one is still on its way, so several of these writes are alive at once — a dozen when a workout logged without signal is replayed. `useOptimisticSetLogMutation` in [`hooks/useSetLogs.js`](../src/hooks/useSetLogs.js), behind the five set-log hooks, therefore never handles a list. `onMutate` merges `buildPatch(variables)` onto the row (`writeRow`) and returns `{ written, previous }` — the patch, and what those fields held before; that context is stored with every queued write, so it is a few values and not the cached lists. `onError` takes back field by field, and only a field that still holds what this write put there (`Object.is`): a newer write to the same row — the RPE given after a tick that is then refused, a "failed" that wrote over the tick — and every other row keep what they show. `onSuccess` writes the row the database answered into the cache, which is what puts a row right again when a read sent before the write came back without it; it is skipped while another write to the same row is pending (`hasPendingSibling` — found by `logId`, told apart by the identity of the variables object), because that write's optimistic state is newer than this answer and its own answer carries both. `writeRow` answers `undefined` to `setQueriesData` for a list that does not hold the row: a list that is written, even with the same rows, is stamped as read just now, and the lists of the other sessions would look fresh after every tap of this one. Restoring a snapshot of the lists on error is the bug this bullet is about: it took every set ticked since off the screen. The cases are in `src/hooks/useSetLogs.optimistic.test.jsx`, whose requests stay open until the test answers them one by one.
- **The set-log hooks read nothing again by themselves.** They set `onMutate`, `onError` and `onSuccess`, and no `onSettled`: the read of `['set-logs']` once the last set-log write has settled is the default of their mutation key (`refreshAfter` in [`lib/offlineMutations.js`](../src/lib/offlineMutations.js)), which also serves a write replayed after a relaunch and stands down while a replay is being refused. An `onSettled` on the hook would replace that default for every write made live — and a read made between two queued writes comes back without the second one. Two things follow. The `onSuccess` of the hook is the only handler of that name for these keys: an `onSuccess` registered in the defaults of a set-log key would never run for a write made live. And the default finds its mutation by the identity of the variables object, so the hook hands React Query ONE object per write (`atTheTap` builds it, nothing copies it afterwards). `useAddStudentSet`, `useRemoveStudentSet` and `useEnsureSetLogs` are not on these keys and still invalidate `['set-logs']` themselves: they add or remove rows.
- **The moment of a set is put into the variables by the hook, when `mutate` is called.** `useOptimisticSetLogMutation` returns its own `mutate` / `mutateAsync`, which add `at` (ISO) to the variables before React Query sees them — `mutate` is called in the handler of the tap or of the swipe, so that is the gesture. `SetRow` does not pass it and its tests pin `{ logId, done }`. A caller that does pass `at` keeps it. `useToggleSetDone` and `useSetFailed` build their optimistic patch from the same value (`patchForDone(done, at)`, `patchForFailed(failed, at)`), and so do `useSetRpe` and `useLogActual` when their variables say `done: true` (`patchForRpe(rpe, { done, at })`, `patchForActual(reps, weightKg, { done, at })`, which the mutation functions call too, with `tapTime(at, madeAt)`), so the row on screen and the row sent carry one timestamp. A new set-log hook goes through the helper, or it sends writes that the mutation layer can only date by `state.submittedAt`. Tests: `src/hooks/useSetLogs.tapTime.test.jsx`.
- **Opening a session starts no write, and nothing that could wait for a network.** `useEnsureSetLogs(sessionId, slots, logs, { enabled })` in [`hooks/useSetLogs.js`](../src/hooks/useSetLogs.js) completes the sets of the session the athlete has open — a prescribed set without its row is added by `ensure_set_logs(p_session_id)` — and it is the hook, not the page, that decides whether anything is sent. Five rules. (1) **Nothing is missing, nothing is sent**: `missingSets` counts, for every exercise the page shows, the numbers from 1 to `sets` without a row among the sets the page has read (`logs`, `undefined` until they are — a list that is not there yet is not an empty one). It is the rule of the function, word for word: a row on a number is a row, an athlete's extra set included. The two must stay one rule — a set the device counts missing and the database does not is a call at every opening. (2) **Nothing is started without a network**: the effect asks `useOnlineStatus()` AND `onlineManager.isOnline()`, the second because it is the one that parks a mutation instead of sending it. A mutation started offline does not fail, it is paused: it is a "change waiting to sync" over a workout in which nothing was entered, and a write the queue cannot replay after a relaunch — its `mutationFn` is not one `registerOfflineMutationDefaults` knows. So this mutation has no `mutationKey`, is not in `MUTATION_KEYS`, and is never started to wait; when the network returns the effect runs again. (3) **The device says whether to ask, the database what is missing**: the variables are `{ sessionId }` and the request is `supabase.rpc('ensure_set_logs', { p_session_id })`. No number of sets and no target leaves the device, so a set the coach removed since the device read the session cannot come back. (4) **Each list of missing sets is asked for once per opening of the page** (`asked`, a `Set` of `sessionId|slot:number,…` held in a ref, emptied when the network goes): an answer that changes nothing on the device must not be asked for again. (5) **A refusal is silent** (`meta: { skipErrorToast: true }`): the athlete asked for nothing, and the sets that exist are on screen. The page switches the hook off (`enabled: false`) on a session it shows read-only — the function refuses a past block and an archived session, and rows added to a reviewed one would be sets the coach never read — and while the session or its sets are being read again (`isFetching`): "missing" is counted on two reads, which are only worth comparing once both have answered. Tests: `src/hooks/useSetLogs.ensure.test.jsx` (the hook, with the device offline, `onlineManager` offline, and each alone), `src/components/student/SessionView.offline.test.jsx` (the page on its real reads next to `OfflineBanner`: a session opened without a network leaves the mutation cache empty).
- **The answer of `ensure_set_logs` is what is read again.** More than `0`: `['set-logs', sessionId]`, which has new rows. `0`: the database is short of nothing where the device counted a set missing, so the device's copy is behind — `['session', sessionId]` (its numbers of sets) and `['set-logs', sessionId]` are both invalidated, after which the two agree and nothing is asked. The read of the sets is left out while a set-log write is pending (`isMutating({ mutationKey: ['set-log'] })`): made between two queued writes it comes back without the second, and the read that follows the last of them (`refreshAfter` in `lib/offlineMutations.js`) brings the added rows as well. No other key is touched, and nothing is read again after a refusal.
- **A database without `ensure_set_logs` is served by the hook itself, and still from the database's exercises.** On `PGRST202` — and on no other error: a refusal of the function is not a missing function — `insertMissingSetLogs(sessionId)` reads `exercise_slots` of the session (`id, sets, reps, duration_seconds, weight_kg, rest_seconds`) and the `set_logs` of those exercises, and inserts the missing rows with the targets of their exercise, under the athlete's own policies. It answers the number of rows like the function, so what is read again is the same. It takes nothing from the cache either. It is an athlete's own `INSERT` of prescribed rows with targets — the write the contract migration of review L6 (Checkpoint Z) is there to close: the migration and the removal of this path go together.
- **A confirmation, its undo and a note for the coach are on screen at the tap, in every read that shows them.** They are made at the end of a workout, in a gym: the write waits for a network more often than not, sometimes until the app has been closed and opened again. So `onMutate` writes everything the athlete is shown about it, and for a confirmation that is three reads, not one: `['session-confirmation', sessionId]` (the card on the session page), `['my-confirmed-session-ids', …]` (which session is "Next" on the training list, the place in the block, the "Done" drawer) and `['student-program-details', …]` (`sessions.performed_at` — the day that dates and orders the drawer and feeds the greeting; `performedAtOf` writes it the way `sync_session_performed_at` will, `<performed_on>T00:00:00+00:00`, or the moment of the confirmation when no day was sent, and `writeTrainingListSession` in [`hooks/useStudentProgramDetails.js`](../src/hooks/useStudentProgramDetails.js) finds the session in the list and in any past block that was opened). Written into the first alone, the session that had just been finished stayed the "Next" one of the list, with its Start button, until the confirmation reached the database (P25). `useUnconfirmSession` does the reverse on the same three; `useSaveSlotComment` writes the note — trimmed, as the database will hold it — into `['slot-comments', sessionId]`, or takes it out for an empty body. Whoever adds a read that shows "this session is done" adds it to these `onMutate`, and to `CONFIRMATION_READS` in `lib/offlineMutations.js` for the read that follows. Tests: `src/hooks/useSessionConfirmation.optimistic.test.jsx` and the second half of `src/hooks/useSlotComments.test.jsx` (requests that stay open until the test answers them), `src/components/student/SessionView.confirm.test.jsx` (the session page and the training list on the real hooks, without a network, across a relaunch and when the network returns).
- **A list the device does not hold is not made by a guess, and the one row it knows is.** `['my-confirmed-session-ids']` and the block are written only where an entry already holds data: a list of confirmed sessions made of the one just confirmed would put every other session of the block back to "not done". `['session-confirmation', sessionId]` is made, as before — it is one row, and a session read ahead by the training list has never had its confirmation read. So are the notes of a session whose notes were never read, with the one note the athlete has just written, and that entry is flagged at once (`invalidateQueries({ exact: true, refetchType: 'none' })`) so that it is read as soon as it can be. The two lists therefore have to be in memory still when the athlete confirms, an hour after leaving the page that reads them — nothing on the session page observes them. That is the `gcTime` of the persisted roots (`PERSIST_MAX_AGE_MS` in [`lib/queryClient.js`](../src/lib/queryClient.js), held by `src/lib/queryClient.cache.test.js`): with React Query's five minutes they are collected during the workout, the confirmation is written into nothing, and the list the athlete comes back to has the session as "Next" again. The tests of the confirmation build their clients with `gcTime: Infinity` and would not see that default go.
- **A write that failed takes back what it put on screen — unless a newer write to the same thing is pending.** Confirm, undo, confirm again without a network is three writes, and what the athlete sees is the state after the last of them: `hasNewerWrite` in [`hooks/useSessionConfirmation.js`](../src/hooks/useSessionConfirmation.js) (any other pending write of the two confirmation keys on that session) and `hasNewerSave` in [`hooks/useSlotComments.js`](../src/hooks/useSlotComments.js) (same exercise) make the `onError` of an earlier one stand down. These writes leave one at a time, in the order they were made (the `offline-writes` scope), so another pending one is always a later one. Otherwise `takeBack` restores what the context holds, which is a few values and not the lists — the confirmation as it was, `list: 'added' | 'removed' | null` (so a session that was already in the list is not taken out of it), and the day with what it replaced, put back only where the session still holds what this write put there. Two traps. `setQueryData(key, undefined)` writes nothing: a confirmation that had never been read is restored as `previous ?? null`, or the card stays on "Session confirmed" after a confirmation the database refused. And none of this runs for a write restored after a relaunch — it has the defaults of its key and no hook: what puts the screen right then is the read that follows it (`refreshAfter`), except while a replay is being refused: a confirmation the database turns down at that moment stays on the card and in the list until their reads go stale.
- **A guess is written through `writeCached`, never with `setQueryData` alone.** `setQueryData` stamps the entry as read just now and takes its "to be read again" flag off. For an optimistic update that is one statement too many: the training list an offline confirmation was written into would count as fresh for two more minutes — not read again when the athlete comes back to it on a network, after a workout during which the coach may have rewritten the block — and an entry invalidated without a network would forget that it is owed a read. `writeCached(queryClient, queryKey, update)` in [`lib/cacheWrite.js`](../src/lib/cacheWrite.js) writes every entry the key matches with the `dataUpdatedAt` it had and puts the flag back, skips an entry that holds nothing, and leaves an entry alone when `update` answers `undefined` or the same data. `['session-confirmation', sessionId]` is the exception that stays on `setQueryData` — it has to be made — and the defaults read it again when the write settles. Tests: `src/lib/cacheWrite.test.js`.
- **"Waits for a network" is asked of the queue, and `isPending` is not it.** A write parked offline is `pending` for as long as the device has no network — hours. Three things follow in [`SessionView.jsx`](../src/components/student/SessionView.jsx). (1) A button is held by a write that is BEING SENT, `isPending && !isPaused` (`confirmInFlight`, `undoInFlight`): keyed on `isPending`, "Confirm session" stayed switched off and on "Confirming…" for a session confirmed and then un-confirmed in the gym, and "Undo confirmation" after an undo followed by a new confirm. (2) The line "Saved on this device. It will be sent as soon as you're back online." (`sessionConfirm.waiting`, `role="status"`, inside the confirmed card) comes from `useConfirmationWaiting(sessionId)`: a `useIsMutating` over the confirm key, paused, for that session — read from the mutation cache and not from `useConfirmSession()`, because the confirmation is usually still waiting after the page was left and after a relaunch, where it is a mutation restored from IndexedDB that no hook of this launch has seen. (3) "Paused" alone is not "offline": React Query also calls a write paused while it stands behind another one of its scope — a note being saved — for a few hundred milliseconds on a working network. So the hook also asks `useOnlineStatus()` and `onlineManager.isOnline()`, the second because it is the one that parks a write.
- **The confirm dialog closes at the tap, and the field of a note when it is left — neither waits for an answer.** The page behind them shows the result from that moment, and the answer can be hours away: a parked write is neither a success nor a failure, and both used to sit open on it (the dialog on "Confirming…" with Cancel switched off, `onClose` ignored). `handleConfirm` calls `mutate` and closes; the buttons of the dialog are never disabled. What was typed is kept for the case the write fails. The dialog's note leaves its field in the `onSuccess` of the call, so after a refusal — the hook has put "Confirm session" back — the dialog opens on it. That answer takes out of the field what it carried and nothing else (`current === written`): it can come hours after the tap, to an athlete who has taken the confirmation back, opened the dialog again and is writing the note of the next one. [`SlotCommentBox.jsx`](../src/components/student/SlotCommentBox.jsx) keeps what a failed save carried (`unsaved`, set by the `onError` of the call) and opens on it next time; it does not reopen by itself, which on a phone is a keyboard coming up in the middle of a set. The box fills its draft when it OPENS (`openEditor`), not by an effect on the note: the note changes under an open field for other reasons than typing — a save that is taken back, a read that comes in — and the effect replaced what was being typed.
- **An exercise has one note control.** One exercise is shown at a time (the Parc session screen), and [SessionExercise.jsx](../src/components/student/SessionExercise.jsx) renders [SlotCommentBox.jsx](../src/components/student/SlotCommentBox.jsx) under its sets: « Add a note for the coach », or the note signed « Your note for the coach » with « Edit the note »; its field saves when it is left. A superset has one note, on its lead slot, shown on each exercise of the pairing. A read-only session (`isReadOnly`) shows the note, and no way to change it. Tests: `student/SlotCommentBox.test.jsx`, `student/SessionView.test.jsx` (the superset's shared note, none to write when reviewed).
- **The confirm dialog says what is confirmed, and asks twice when nothing is logged.** It opens with "Sets logged: N of M" (`sessionConfirm.summary`): the sets the athlete has dealt with — done, failed, skipped, or under an exercise they skipped — which is what the progress bar counts (`resolvedCount`), so a session at 100% is not confirmed as "10 of 12". With M > 0 and N = 0 the first tap on Confirm sends nothing: it shows `sessionConfirm.nothingLogged` (`role="alert"`) and the button becomes "Confirm anyway"; closing the dialog forgets that it asked. A session can be confirmed without a set — trained and not ticked, or not trained and said so in the note — but not by a slip of the thumb: the coach is notified and the session leaves "Next" (live item N8). A session with no set at all has nothing to count and is confirmed in one tap.
- **A test that leaves a write parked empties its client before the network is put back.** `onlineManager` is one object for a whole test file, and the page or the hook a test rendered is still mounted in `afterEach`: `onlineManager.setOnline(true)` there sends whatever the test left waiting. That request gets no answer, or one nobody reads; and with the retry policy of the queued writes (`mutationRetry`, `mutationRetryDelay`) a write that got no answer is sent again one, two and four seconds later — inside another test, into whatever stands for the database there. `SessionView.confirm.test.jsx` failed that way in half of its runs or more: the confirmation its first test leaves in the gym took the refusal a later test had set for its own. The three test files of the confirmation and of the notes that take the network away (`SessionView.confirm.test.jsx`, `useSessionConfirmation.optimistic.test.jsx`, `useSlotComments.test.jsx`) keep the clients they make and call `clear()` on each in `afterEach`, before `setOnline(true)`.
- **`['exercise-library']` is one key for two requests, and has to stay one key.** [`useExerciseLibrary`](../src/hooks/useExerciseLibrary.js) is shared: the coach's request carries `coach_id = <their id>`, the athlete's carries no filter (theirs would match nothing, and empty the swap picker of `SessionView` and the picker of `StudentProgramAuthor`). The key is the same for both because `exercise-library` is a persisted root and the entry an athlete's device already holds is what names their exercises offline: a key per user or per role would leave that entry unread. The price is that the cache can hold a list fetched without the filter — restored from a device that ran an earlier client, or read before the profile, and with it the role, had loaded (`RoleGate` renders its page while the role is unknown) — and nothing refetches it when the role arrives, since the key does not change. The hook's `select` therefore applies the coach rule to whatever the cache holds. Do not move that rule into the query function alone, and read the library through the hook: `queryClient.getQueryData(['exercise-library'])` returns the list before `select`.
